Introduction
The Weil group of the rational number field Q is a locally compact Hausdorff group WQ
which refines the absolute Galois group GQ, via a surjective homomorphism
WQ ↠GQ
with connected kernel. It determines an analogous Weil group WK for an arbitrary number
field K, by taking the pullback along the inclusion GK →GQ.
This Weil group was constructed by Weil in [67]. The motivation came from L-functions,
or more specifically, from the desire to unite two different classes of L-functions: the Artin L-
functions attached to complex Galois representations, and the Hecke L-functions attached to
Hecke characters A×
K/K× →C×. These classes were known to agree on a common locus, for a
deep reason: a one-dimensional representation of GK, which corresponds to a homomorphism
Gab
K →C×,
1
arXiv:2605.11950v1  [math.NT]  12 May 2026


## Page 2


can be composed with the Artin map A×
K/K× ↠Gab
K to get a Hecke character. This sets up
a 1-1 correspondence between one-dimensional Galois representations and Hecke characters
with finite image, and Artin reciprocity says that the corresponding Artin and Hecke L-
functions are equal.
Not all Artin representations are one dimensional (or built from such via direct sums and
inductions), and, on the other hand, not all Hecke characters have finite image. So we have
two separate worlds, though with substantial common overlap. Then it makes sense to look
for an even larger world containing both of them. One such is provided by the notion of
complex representation of the Weil group. The key point is that the Artin map lifts to an
isomorphism
W ab
K ≃A×
K/K×
for any number field K. Weil was able to define Artin-style L-functions attached to Weil
group representations, and he showed that Hecke L-functions exactly correspond to the case
of one-dimensional representations via the above lifted Artin map.
A beautiful aspect of Weil groups is that although the motivation for them comes from
analytic number theory and representation theory, their construction involves a completely
different set of ideas: namely it requires, and indeed encapsulates (see [64]), the cohomological
approach to class field theory. The key idea is to view the absolute Galois group GQ not in
the naive manner, as the inverse limit of its finite quotients (indexed by number fields K
inside a fixed Q, as usual), but as the inverse limit of coarser terms
GQ = lim
←Ð
K
Gal(Kab/Q),
which themselves sit in short exact sequences of groups
1 →Gab
K →Gal(Kab/Q) →Gal(K/Q) →1
with finite quotient and abelian kernel. To build the Weil group we want to replace Gab
K in
this short exact sequence by its refinement A×
K/K×.
Here comes the miraculous “coincidence”: short exact sequences of the desired type are
classified, up to isomorphism, by group cohomology classes
u ∈H2(Gal(K/Q);A×
K/K×),
and cohomological class field theory exactly produces canonical such classes u = uK/Q. One
has to take some care to put the resulting group extensions into an inverse system, but in
the end more information from cohomological class field theory guarantees that it can be
done, see [3].
Our perspective in this article is that one should not stop with the Weil group. Other
cohomological considerations, originating in work of Tate, suggest the need to further refine
the Weil group. What we will see is that, contrary to naive hypothesis, a number field should
not be a “K(π,1)”: besides the “fundamental group” (the Weil group), there should also be
higher homotopy groups.
2


## Page 3


Formally speaking, the first step has to be saying exactly what kind of object we mean to
construct. We want something that has homotopy groups, but where these homotopy groups
are not abstract groups, but rather topological groups (in fact locally compact Hausdorff
groups, like WQ, which are very close to being compact). Thankfully, in joint work with
Peter Scholze we defined and proved some basic properties of a class of objects, called
condensed anima, which can play such a role.
Remark 1.1. I often get asked to explain the terminology “condensed” and the terminology
“anima”, which were decided on by myself and Peter Scholze. My explanations are usually
something like the following.
1. The idea of condensed sets is that one encodes topological structures as being built
from profinite sets via colimits. Profinite sets tend to look like Cantor sets.
Why the name “condensed”? If one starts with an abstract set, then instead of specify-
ing a collection of open subsets, as is required to describe a topology in the traditional
sense, one specifies which families of points are allowed to “condense” together to form
a compact Hausdorff space. These compact Hausdorff spaces can in turn be exploded
into profinite sets, because every compact Hausdorff space is a quotient of a profinite
set by a profinite equivalence relation.
2. An anima is just a homotopy type, but viewed as an object in its natural ambient
∞-category rather than its 1-categorical shadow (the classical homotopy category).
Why the name “anima”? There is a two-fold motivation. First, one has the following
intuition described by Beilinson in [4]. A set is static in the sense that its points are
stuck where they are: they cannot move around within the set. But if we have an
anima A, with underlying set π0A, then a point of A is free to move around within
its connected component: as Beilinson says, the points of A are naturally “animated”.
Second, an anima is something like a soul. If we have, say, a CW complex, then its
associated anima is what remains of the CW complex when it is stripped of its worldly
(point-set) incarnation.
A condensed anima is an object which has both aspects, but in an independent way. Thus
it has both a “topological” aspect and a “homotopy-theoretic” aspect, and the topology is not
collapsed to homotopy theory. Thus for example the Eilenberg-Maclane space K(R,n) on
the real numbers with its usual structure of topological abelian group exists as a condensed
anima, and it is different from the ordinary anima K(Rδ,n) and ∗.
The former is its
underlying anima, and the latter is its homotopy type.
Our problem is therefore well-posed: we want to find a condensed anima with fundamental
group WQ and nontrivial higher homotopy groups.
The main thing we want from this
condensed anima is that its cohomological properties should be “better” than those of WQ
or GQ (or rather of BWQ = K(WQ,1) or BGQ = K(GQ,1), corresponding to the fact that
we’re talking about group cohomology).
We state the theorem now, and give more precise motivation afterwards.
3


## Page 4


Theorem 1.2. There exists a condensed anima XQ with the following properties:
1. The condensed set π0XQ is ∗.
2. The condensed group π1XQ is isomorphic to the Weil group WQ of the rational numbers.
3. For n > 1, the condensed abelian group πnXQ is compact Hausdorff.
4. For any number field K, if XK →XQ denotes the corresponding finite etale cover, then
Hi(XK;R/Z) = 0 for i > 1.
To motivate the crucial property 4, recall that the essence of Weil groups is the existence of
canonical isomorphisms W ab
K ≃A∗
K/K∗refining the Artin map. Equivalently, by Pontryagin
duality, we can phrase this as a cohomology calculation:
H1(BWK;R/Z) = (A∗
K/K∗)∨,
where we use (−)∨to denote Pontryagin duality.
However, the higher cohomology groups H>1(BWK;R/Z) do not all vanish. This can
be interpreted as saying that the isomorphism W ab
K ≃A∗
K/K× is not “robust”. The purpose
of the XK is to fix this: we add higher homotopy groups to BWK in order to make its
abelianization correspond to A×
K/K× in some “true” or “derived” sense.
Remark 1.3. There are Weil groups associated not just to number fields, but also func-
tion fields over finite fields and local fields. In non-archimedean cases (function fields and
nonarchimedean local fields), the higher cohomology above does vanish by a theorem of Tate
([60]), so we will not be changing anything in non-archimedean cases. In other words, for
nonarchimedean K we will have XK = BWK.
Remark 1.4. One can think of the relationship between XK and BWK = K(WK,1) as being
analogous to the relationship between Sn and K(Z,n) in algebraic topology: the spheres Sn
have simple cohomology (they are “Moore spaces”), while the K(Z,n) have simple homotopy
(they are “Eilenberg-Maclane spaces”).
Serre in [56] explained how one can build spheres from Eilenberg-Maclane spaces, up to
homotopy equivalence, by an inductive procedure, building up the Postnikov tower of Sn.
We apply this same procedure to build XQ from BWQ = K(WQ,1), though needless to say
there are substantial technical complications because of the nontrivial fundamental group
(and, to a somewhat lesser extent, because we have to do everything in the condensed world).
Remark 1.5. The question of how (and in what sense) to put a geometric structure on XQ
is tantalizing. Presumably, XQ should be, in some vague sense, the condensed homotopy
type underlying the space of closed points in the global analog of the Fargues-Fontaine curve
(whose existence has been hypothesized by Scholze), analogously to how, in [20], BWQp
appears (in some sense) as the homotopy type of Div1, which is the space of closed points
in the Fargues-Fontaine curve.
Another interesting question is that of how to calculate the higher homotopy groups of
XQ. In principle our construction gives a method analogous to Serre’s from his thesis, but
we do not investigate this in detail. For π2, see Lemma 7.11.
4


## Page 5


Our main point is that the cohomology of these XK somehow “improves” the usual Galois
cohomology. Notably, we have the following, which for the moment we state imprecisely:
1. The cohomological dimension of XK, and its variants XK,S with restricted ramification
at a nonempty set of places S, is always 2, independently of whether K has a real place
or not and independently of Leopoldt’s conjecture.
2. There are local variants of the XK, and there are versions of global and local Poitou-
Tate duality for these Weil-Moore anima in which the archimedean and non-archimedean
places contribute in a uniform way. (At nonarchimedean places we have XKν = BWKν,
so there is nothing new, but at archimedean places there is a change.)
3. A simpler version of Tate’s global Euler characteristic formula holds for these XK,S
(with S finite): the Euler characteristic is always 0, just as in the function field case.
Let us expand on this by describing some idiosyncrasies of Galois cohomology, which
we aim to “repair” by considering the cohomology of the XK instead. We can frame the
discussion in terms of the well-known analogy with 3-manifolds with boundary ([43]). The
main point is that this analogy is slightly broken, for reasons coming from the archimedean
places.
More explicitly, consider Poitou-Tate duality ([63], [42]), which is supposed to be the
analog of Poincar´e duality for manifolds with boundary. Let K be a number field, let p be
a prime, let S be a finite set of places of K containing all archimedean places and all places
above p, and let M be a finite GK,S-module of p-power order. Then the continuous group
cohomology RΓ(BGK,S;M) is finite of p-power order in each degree, and its Pontryagin dual
is described as follows:
RΓ(BGK,S;M)∨≃̃
RΓc(GF,S;M ∨(1)[3]).
Here
̃
RΓc(BGK,S;M) ∶= Fib(RΓ(BGK,S;M) →⊕ν∈S ̃
RΓ(BGKν;M)),
where ̃
RΓ(BGKν;M) denotes the usual continuous group cohomology if ν is nonarchimedean,
and Tate cohomology (of the finite group GKν) if ν is archimedean.
This looks almost like Poincar´e duality for the interior of a compact 3-manifold with
boundary, where we interpret S as parametrizing the boundary components, and we interpret
the Tate twist as the orientation local system. But only “almost”, because at the archimedean
places we use Tate cohomology instead of usual cohomology. For example at complex places
the Tate cohomology vanishes, so somehow we forget those components, and at real places
we just get some 2-torsion groups, but generally in infinitely many degrees, positive and
negative. On the other hand, at nonarchimedean places we use the usual cohomology, and
there local Tate duality looks just like Poincar´e duality for a 2-dimensional closed manifold.
Thus there is an asymmetry between the nonarchimedean and archimedean places, where
the nonarchimedean places fit into the philosophy of a 2-dimensional boundary component
but the archimedean places do not.
5


## Page 6


For a more elementary perspective, recall that the “fundamental class” for Poitou-Tate
duality comes from the famous calculation of the Brauer group of a number field, namely
that it is the kernel of the sum of the invariant maps from the local Brauer groups. At nonar-
chimedean places the invariant map is an isomorphism Br(Fν) ≃Q/Z, but at archimedean
places it is only an injection, with image of order ≤2. Somehow, at the archimedean places
the Brauer group is too small.
At the root of all this is the fact that while the absolute Galois group GQp of Qp is a
big and powerful object (according to Fontaine its p-adic representations can encode a lot
of p-adic Hodge theoretic information), the absolute Galois groups of R and C are too small
to reveal any interesting information, say of Hodge-theoretic nature.
This is partially fixed by passing from Galois groups to Weil groups. The Weil group
of the local field C is C× and the Weil group of R is an extension of GR by C×. So we do
get something bigger, and indeed complex representations of archimedean Weil groups can
be used to encode pure Hodge structures with complex coeficients, see [64]. From a group
cohomology perspective, Weil groups also fix the problem with the Brauer group: if you
calculate H2(BWR;Q/Z(1)) or H2(BWC;Q/Z(1)), you get the full Q/Z; correspondingly,
the Weil version of the global Brauer group is also the “correct” one for an arbitrary number
field K, namely
H2(BWK;Q/Z(1)) = ker(⊕νQ/Z
Σ→Q/Z),
where the direct sum is over all places of K, as was shown by Deligne in [15].
However, there is still a problem with the Weil group cohomology, in higher degrees:
the cohomological dimension of WC and WR is not 2 like in the nonarchimedean case, but
infinite. For example Hi(BWC;Q/Z) gives Q/Z not just in degree 2, but in all nonnegative
even degrees. Indeed the classifying stack BWC has the homotopy type of BS1 ∼CP∞. So
we still don’t have the “correct” 2-dimensional object, which would have the same kind of
Poincar´e duality as the Galois group (or Weil group) of a nonarchimedean local field.
There is a straightforward fix to this, which is to take just the 2-skeleton of CP∞, or in
other words the 2-sphere S2. This of course has 2-dimensional Poincar´e duality. Moreover
we can make GR act on this by the antipodal map on the geometric 2-sphere and this gives
a reasonable 2-dimensional object for R, namely RP2.
In fact this passage to RP2 is also natural from the perspective of Hodge theory, or
more precisely Simpson’s twistor theory ([61]). Indeed S2 is P1(C), and vector bundles on
P1
C equipped with semilinear structure relative to the antipodal involution are at the heart
of Simpson’s reinterpretation of what is a Hodge structure (with R-coefficients over C, this
time). In particular, antipodal twistor structures have Chern classes living in the cohomology
of this 2-skeleton of the real Weil group. Following the discussion of Fargues in [19], this
is analogous to how vector bundles on the Fargues-Fontaine curve have Chern classes with
values in the cohomology of the p-adic Weil group.
Although it has the correct cohomology, the homotopy type (anima) S2 of course has
trivial fundamental group and certainly cannot recover the Weil group C× as a topological
group, so we lost something here. But this is exactly the sort of thing condensed anima are
good for. There is an action of WC = C× on H× by multiplication, hence an action of the
6


## Page 7


condensed group WC on the homotopy type ∣H×∣∼S3, and when we take the quotient we get
something with fundamental group WC but whose cohomology (with discrete coefficients) is
the same as that of S2 (think of the Hopf fibration). The same construction works for the
reals as well, using that WR can be realized as a subgroup of H×.
Thus we get a fairly satisfying picture at the archimedean places. The Weil group admits a
reasonable refinement: a condensed anima whose π1 is the Weil group, and whose cohomology
with discrete coefficients has two dimensional duality exactly like a non-archimedean local
field. (Actually the refinement we give in the body of this paper will be slightly different
than the one mentioned above, but not in a way that makes a difference at the level we are
discussing things here.)
The issue, then is to make a similar refinement for the Weil group of a number field,
compatible with the above story at the archimedean places (and with the usual Weil groups
at non-archimedean places, which are in no need of refinement), such that we get a reasonable
version of Poitou-Tate duality. That is what we accomplish in this article.
To finish this introduction, let’s give a brief tour of some areas of number theory where
Galois (or Weil group) cohomology arises, and then indicate how the refinement we suggest
somehow improves the picture. We will not go into any more details about these points in
the body of the paper; we discuss them here just for motivation.
1. First and most immediately, there is the “Weil-etale” perspective on special values of
zeta functions developed in [39], where the exact same issues as discussed above arise.
Lichtenbaum considers a Weil group version of the compactly supported cohomology
of the ring of integers OF of a number field F, with coefficents in Z, and shows that if
we truncate to degrees ≤3, then this gives an object in Perf(Z), and that moreover, its
base-change to Perf(R) admits an expression of the form fib(V
Φ→V ) for some canonical
V ∈Perf(R) with canonical endomorphism Φ. On the level of K-theory, it follows that
the point in K(Z) defined by this cohomology promotes to the homotopy fiber of
K(Z) →K(R), whence we get a natural element of the group K1(R)/K1(Z) = R×/Z×
of nonzero real numbers up to sign.
The result of [39] is that this nonzero real number (up to sign) is equal to the the
leading coefficient of ζF at s = 0, with the order of vanishing being given by the
Euler characteristic of V . This is a compelling reformulation of the Dirichlet class
number formula. However, in high degrees the compactly supported cohomology with
Z-coefficients is nonzero and huge, see [21]. Thus the ad hoc truncation used above
was necessary, and the picture is somewhat spoiled. The trouble again is that the Weil
group of a number field or of an archimedean local field has unwanted cohomology in
degrees > 3. This is fixed by the object we propose, so Lichtenbaum’s idea goes through
directly and without truncation.
2. Similarly (and relatedly), turning towards p-adic coefficients, we can consider Kato’s
generalized Iwasawa main conjecture ([34]). Let p be a prime, Λ a commutative pro-
p-finite ring and let M be a lisse Λ-sheaf on Spec(Z[1/S])et, where S is a finite set of
7


## Page 8


prime numbers with p ∈S. Kato conjectured that associated to this data is a canonical
zeta element, which is a particular isomorphism
ζM ∶detΛ(RΓc(Spec(Z[1/S])et;M)) ≃Λ.
Besides natural formal properties, these zeta elements are, crucially, supposed to satisfy
a more nuanced axiom which says that when M comes from a motive, there is a precise
relation with special values of (complex) L-functions.
Notably, here RΓc is defined in the completely naive way:
RΓc(Spec(Z[1/S])et;M) ∶= Fib(RΓ(Spec(Z[1/S])et;M) →⊕ν∈S∪{∞}RΓ(Spec(Qν)et;M)).
We use usual etale (or Galois) cohomology everywhere, not Tate cohomology or Weil
group cohomology. We do have
RΓc(Spec(Z[1/S])et;M) ∈Perf(Λ)
for all p,S,Λ,M as above, which shows that the determinant in the statement is well-
defined. Moreover, Tate’s global Euler characteristic formula has a reformulation which
says that the above perfect complex always has Euler characteristic 0 (a “K0” com-
panion to Kato’s conjecture, which implicitly has to do with K1, or rather τ≤1K).
It seems that here, in contrast to the the situations described above, everything behaves
perfectly logically and there is no need for a refinement.
Yet, we are proposing a
refinement, so we should check what it does. Thankfully, everything makes sense: in
this setting, we will show that there is a natural isomorphism
RΓc(Spec(Z[1/S])et;M)
∼→RΓc(XQ,S;M).
That is, with these kinds of coefficients, compactly supported etale (or Galois) cohomol-
ogy is the same as compactly supported Weil-Moore cohomology, so Kato’s conjecture
can equivalently be formulated in the Weil-Moore context.
Moreover, once we are in the Weil-Moore world, we gain something: by Poitou-Tate
duality for Weil-Moore anima, we can also reformulate Kato’s conjecture as positing
trivializations
detΛ RΓ(XQ,S;M) ≃Λ,
for any M and Λ as above, here using the non-compactly supported cohomology of the
Weil-Moore anima. More precisely, the two conjectures are equivalent under switching
M ↔M ∨(1).
This reformulation should constitute a modest improvement. Actually, Kato remarks
that for the purposes of producing such zeta isomorphisms it is helpful to pass to the
Poitou-Tate dual perspective of usual (not compactly supported) cohomology, because
that is where “special elements” like cyclotomic units naturally live. However, since
as we mentioned above, classical Poitou-Tate duality has some strange aspects at the
archimedean places, this means that Kato has to introduce an archimedean correction
factor to his conjecture, and moreover the prime p = 2 requires extra attention. When
we pass to the Weil-Moore setting, this becomes much simpler, as stated above.
8


## Page 9


3. The previous points had to do with direct uses for the cohomology of the XK,S. One
can say with reasonable justification that this on this linear level, XK,S just provides a
reorganization of known aspects of Galois cohomology. However, the fact that it arises
from the non-linear object XK,S gives extra information, and we will now explain that
this information is relevant in the Langlands program.
More precisely, we mean the Langlands program with mod p or p-adic coefficients, for
some fixed prime p; thus we avoid having to contend with the conjectural Langlands
group ([38], [2]), which appears when working with the classical C-coefficients due to the
fact that even the Weil group WQ does not have nearly enough complex representations
to match automorphic phenomena. But for mod p coefficients this is not a concern.
Our basic claim is that on the “spectral side” of the Langlands correspondence for
number fields, one should replace the notion of Galois representation by the notion of
a Weil-Moore representation: that is, a finite rank local system on XK. At the naive
level this gives nothing new: as
τ≤1XK = BWK,
a Weil-Moore representation is the same thing as a Weil group representation. If the
coefficient ring R for our representations is discrete or p-adic, it’s also the same as a
Galois representation, because WK/W ○
K = GK.
More precisely, the above reasoning holds when R is a classical commutative ring, i.e.
when R = π0R. If R has nontrivial derived structure, then the notion of Weil-Moore
representation differs from the notion of Weil group representation which in turn differs
from the notion of Galois group representation.
Let us be a bit more precise about this. For a group scheme ̂G over Λ = Z/pnZ, we can
consider, for any animated commutative Λ-algebra R, the anima of maps of condensed
anima
XF →(B ̂G)(R),
where the target is discrete (as a condensed anima, meaning it is just an ordinary
anima). This defines a formal derived stack XF = XF, ̂
G over Spec(Λ) organizing “defor-
mations of Weil-Moore representations”. Then these XF, ̂
G provide a derived structure
on the usual Galois deformation functors, or Wang-Erickson stacks, [66], which is dif-
ferent than the naive derived structure considered by Galatius-Venkatesh in [25].
To see what this might give in practice, let’s turn to the remarkable conjectures of
Emerton-Gee-Hellmann and Zhu, see [17] and [68]. These posit a certain “spectral
decomposition” of the compactly supported cohomology of Shimura varieties. In this
context, to give a spectral decomposition of a vector space means to find stack of “pa-
rameters” and an Ind-coherent sheaf on that stack whose global sections identify with
the vector space. (This rather refined notion of what it means to give a spectral decom-
position arose in the geometric Langlands program, see [23] and [1], as an extension
and reinterpretation of the work of Lafforgue [37]. We refer to [48], especially Remark
5.4, for a particularly cogent explanation.)
9


## Page 10


In the case of the above-mentioned conjectures, the stack of parameters is quite liter-
ally a moduli stack of L-parameters, and the Ind-coherent sheaf is directly described
(conjecturally) in terms of categorical local Langlands at the non-archimedean places!
Actually, this is a bit of an oversimplification, because one needs to somehow take
care of the archimedean places as well. We refer to [17] and upcoming work of Davis-
Emerton-Vilonen for more details. But this is precisely where the Weil-Moore versions
can potentially help, in making the archimedean places more directly analogous to the
non-archimedean places. As a bonus, one can formulate a more general conjecture
describing the compactly supported cohomology of an arbitrary adelic quotient, at
least modulo standard technicalities having to do with the Hasse priniciple.
As a preliminary to this, one needs to understand categorical local Langlands at
the archimedean places, with discrete coefficients, analogously to the categorical lo-
cal Langlands conjectures at non-archimedean places discussed in [20], [68], [17]. In
fact, in conversations with Peter Scholze several years ago we came up with a proposal
for what this archimedean analogue should be: namely, following Ben-Zvi–Nadler ([5]),
it should simply be Betti geometric Langlands for P1
C at complex places, and a version
of Betti geometric Langlands for the twistor line TwR at real places. See also [49],
which is a more involved version of the same idea aimed at real local Langlands with
complex coefficients instead of discrete coefficients.
The Betti geometric Langlands of Ben-Zvi–Nadler ([5]) for real curves should look
roughly as follows (we will explain the subtleties later). Let X be a smooth proper
curve over R such that X(R) = ∅; on the dual side, the same data is encoded in the
closed conformal (not necessarily oriented or connected) surface
∣X∣= X(C)/Gal(C/R).
This ∣X∣is equivalently the “moduli space of closed points in X” and is the analog of
Div1 from the Fargues-Scholze setting, [20].
Let G be a reductive group over R. On the automorphic side, one takes sheaves (of
Λ-modules) with nilpotent singular support on the real points of the moduli stack of G
bundles on X, and on the spectral side one takes Ind-coherent sheaves with nilpotent
singular support on the stack of ̂G-local systems on ∣X∣. Note that if X is a curve over
C viewed as a (geometrically disconnected) curve over R, this reduces to the usual
Betti geometric Langlands from [5].
If TwR denotes the twistor line, the nontrivial real form of P1, then ∣TwR ∣= RP2 and
this notion of Λ-local system exactly matches the notion of a Weil-Moore representation
with Λ-coefficients over Spec(R). Thus what we are proposing is that our suggested
modifications of the Galois/Weil group are exactly what is required to make Betti
geometric Langlands directly relevant to arithmetic Langlands for number fields (not
just relevant by analogy).
However, it’s important to note some technical subtleties in this situation. The closed
surface ∣X∣can be non-orientable, and indeed it is non-orientable for our primary
10


## Page 11


example X = TwR. This means we must use the C-group ([10]) instead of the L-group
when talking about local systems for the dual group on the curve. More precisely, the
usual action of Gal(C/R) = π1∣X∣on ̂G coming from Langlands duality gives rise to a
local system of reductive groups on ∣X∣, allowing to define a twisted notion of ̂G-local
system. This is the twist which corresponds to the usual notion of L-parameters, but
it needs to be twisted even more (or differently): there is a canonical map µ2 →Z( ̂G)
(see [7]) which means we can further twist the notion of ̂G-local system by a µ2-gerbe
on ∣X∣, and the gerbe we take is the gerbe of square roots of the Tate twist. This
extra-twisted (C-group) version is exactly the version of local system which arises from
considering geometric Satake in families over ∣X∣(viewed as the space parametrizing
closed points of X), compare [20].
Even more annoyingly (though somehow also equivalently, but on the dual side), the
canonical bundle Ω1 on TwR does not admit a square root, and this precludes, at least
for certain G such as G = SL2, the existence of a Whittaker sheaf, which is used to pin
down the geometric Langlands equivalence. In geometric Langlands over C, this is just
an annoying technicality which means that geometric Langlands is not canonical (one
needs to fix Whittaker data to make it canonical). For geometric Langlands over R,
however, it is a more serious issue, because when there is no Whittaker data, geometric
Langlands simply cannot hold in the usual formulation. To get around this, one can
(and must) make a further twist. We could either twist the automorphic category as
described in [6] C.7 to get a canonical Whittaker sheaf, or else twist the spectral side
to lose the structure sheaf. The former solution is more symmetric and aesthetically
pleasing, but in the global situation with number fields it is less clear what form the
automorphic Whittaker twist should take, so let us explain the spectral Whittaker
twist instead.
More precisely we twist the category of Ind-coherent sheaves by a certain canonical
µ2-gerbe on the stack of ̂GC-local systems. I believe this µ2-gerbe can be described as
follows (compare [24]): any reductive group admits a canonical µ2-central extension,
see again [7], which implies that a ̂GC-local system on ∣X∣induces a µ2-gerbe on ∣X∣.
Cupping with the gerbe of square roots of the canonical bundle of X and integrating
over ∣X∣we get a µ2-gerbe on the stack of ̂GC-local systems. This should be the gerbe
we use to twist the notion of Ind-coherent sheaf in order to get a geometric Langlands
correspondence which is independent of a choice of Whittaker sheaf.
The above subtleties with Whittaker data should also exist for the categorical local
Langlands with mod p coefficients at nonarchimedean places.
[17] avoids them by
restricting to groups of adjoint type, where the Whittaker twist is trivial (so only the
C-group twist remains).
Now, returning to the global situation, let K be a number field and G a reductive
group over K, and let us say that our goal is to give a spectral decomposition of the
compactly supported cohomology
RΓc(G(AK)/G(K);Λ)
11


## Page 12


of the standard adelic quotient, where we fix our coefficient ring to be Λ = Z/pnZ
for some prime p and n ≥1. Note that compactly supported cohomology is the most
elementary possible variant of cohomology to consider, because it is the only one which
naturally produces a discrete Λ-module on an arbitrary locally compact Hausdorff
space (in the case at hand, G(AK)/G(K)); even better the above compactly supported
cohomology gives an admissible representation of G(AK).
Actually, we need to switch to a slight variant of G(AK)/G(K), namely
(BG)c(K) ∶= Fib((BG)(K) →(BG)(AK)).
In more standard adelic terms this can be described as
(BG)c(K) = ⊔
ξ
Gξ(K)/G(AK)
where ξ runs over a set of representatives for ker1(K,G) with Gξ the corresponding
pure inner twist. Thus, passing from the standard adelic quotient to this (BG)c(K) is
a rather mild modification, and if G satisfies the Hasse principle it is not a modificiation
at all.
We take as known the (semi-)local version of our question, namely the compactly
supported cohomology
RΓc(G(AK);Λ),
as a G(AK)-representation, and seek to describe RΓc((BG)c(K);Λ) in terms of it.

Langlands parameters the C-group sense, defined in terms of the stacks of local C-

There is a global-to-local map

In other words, the Ind-coherent sheaf f !UAK should provide the desired refined spectral
decomposition of the given space of (mod pn) automorphic forms for G. Note that UAK
is built from purely local data, so in this conjecture (as in the similar conjectures in
[17], [68], [1]) all the global information about automorphic forms comes on the dual
side from applying the functor f !. An important technical remark is that the global
sections on the right makes sense because the Whittaker gerbe trivializes on pullback
to XK, essentially because of Hasse reciprocity.
It would be interesting to gain enough understanding of Betti Langlands for TwR to see
that this formulation is compatible with the one in [17] in the case of Shimura varieties.
In particular it would be nice to see the familiar “oddness” of the Galois representations
one gets from known spaces of cohomological automorphic forms, which is put in by
hand in [17], being (at least conjecturally) a consequence of archimedean categorical
local Langlands.
One can similarly translate Zhu’s conjectures on the compactly-supported cohomology
of moduli stacks of shtukas ([68]) from the function field case to the number field
case using these ideas. Rather, we can translate the Galois side of Zhu’s conjectural
isomorphisms, without yet knowing what should go on the automorphic side. In any
case this supports Scholze’s conviction ([52],[48]) that shtukas (and their moduli) should
somehow make sense in the number field case as well.
4. Speaking of number field shtukas, they tie into another motivation for the present work.
For such a notion to exist, there needs to be an object “Ω1
Q/F1”, in some (exotic) sense
a line bundle on Spec(Q), which provides the correct twists when studying behaviour
of shtukas along their locus of modification, analogous to the existing object Ω1
F/Fq in
the function field case.
What can we say about this (undefined) object? Only one thing is currently known:
if we pull back along Spa(Qp) →Spec(Q), then the resulting object “Ω1
Qp/F1” actually
has a meaning, as discussed by Faltings ([18]). It can be analysed like this: first, if
F/Qp is perfectoid and contains all p-power roots of unity, then Fontaine’s calculations
([22]) show that LF/Qp (or rather its natural completion) functorially identifies with the
shifted Tate twist F(1)[1]. On the other hand it’s reasonable to posit that LF/F1 = 0,
because the analog in characteristic p holds. Combining, we see that
“LQp/F1 = Qp(1).”
Here, more formally, Qp(1) is a pro-etale line bundle on Spa(Qp) whose sections over
any large enough pro-etale extension F of Qp canonically identifies with the rank one
F-module F(1). This is indeed the object giving the relevant twists in the picture of
local shtukas with one leg, [53].
13


## Page 14


Keeping with our cohomological theme, although we don’t know in which category the
global “Ω1
Q/F1” should live, we can try to ask what its first Chern class should be, say
with finite coefficients. Again we only know what happens on base-change to Qp. Since
etale cohomology has pro-etale descent, pro-etale vector bundles over Qp have Chern
classes in Galois cohomology just like ordinary vector bundles. For Qp(1) and with
(mod pn) coefficients, the answer was given by Petrov when discussing joint work with
Pan: we have that
c1(Qp(1)) ∈H2(GQp;Z/pnZ(1)) ↦1 ∈Z/pnZ
under the invariant map inv ∶H2(GQp;Z/pnZ(1)) ≃Z/pnZ (the fundamental class
for local Tate duality). On the other hand one can also calculate that with mod ℓn
coefficients for ℓ≠p, we have
c1(Qp(1)) = 0 ∈H2(GQp;Z/ℓnZ(1)).
What does this mean for “c1(Ω1
Q/F1)” in (mod pn) cohomology? Its local invariant at
the prime p is as nontrivial as possible (generator of a cyclic group of order pn), while
its local invariants at primes ℓ≠p are trivial. By reciprocity, this means that its local
invariant at the archimedean place must be as nontrivial as possible, for all p.
Again, the Brauer group of R is too small to accommodate such a Chern class. This
means that c1(Ω1
Q/F1) cannot live in usual Galois cohomology H2(GQ;Z/nZ(1)), be-
cause this involves the too-small Brauer group at the infinite place. But there’s also a
separate issue: the base-change of “Ω1
Q/F1” to the infinite place should not be construed
to be Ω1
R/F1, because that object should vanish (R behaves like a perfectoid).
Both problems are fixed if we make the above-suggested replacement of R by the twistor
line TwR. In fact, something very pleasant happens: the first Chern class
c1(Ω1
TwR /R) ∈H2(TwR;Z/nZ(1))
of the cotangent bundle of TwR maps to the generator −1 ∈Z/pnZ under the invariant
map for all p and n, exactly as required by reciprocity! (Note also that since R counts
as perfectoid, at the heuristic level we have Ω1
TwR /R = Ω1
TwR /F1.) Thus, cohomologically
speaking, everything looks perfect for Ω1
TwR /R to be the correct base-change to the
infinite place of our fictional object Ω1
Q/F1.
This gives the impression that c1(Ω1
Q/F1) really does deserve to exist, and that it should
live in H2(XQ;Z/nZ(1)) where XQ is some kind of “space” replacing Spec(Q) for which
Poitou-Tate duality takes the optimal form as discussed above. In this article we at
least produce XQ as a condensed anima. This is not enough to make sense of Ω1, but
it is enough sense to make sense of its first Chern class.

Background on condensed anima
In this paper we use the formalism of condensed anima, developed by the author and Scholze.
Here we give a rapid overview of some basics of this theory.
The quickest version of the definition is the following.
Definition 2.1. Let Extr denote the category of extremally disconnected compact Hausdorff
spaces. Note that Extr has finite coproducts given by disjoint unions.
The ∞-category of condensed anima is the ∞-category
CondAn = PΣ(Extr)
freely generated by Extr under sifted colimits, as formally encoded in the PΣ-construction of
[40] 5.5.8.
In other words, CondAn is the ∞-category generated by compact projective objects, such
that the full subcategory of compact projective objects in CondAn identifies with Extr.
By construction, CondAn is a full subcategory of presheaves of anima on Extr, namely
it is spanned by those X ∶Extrop →An satisfying the following two conditions:
1. If I is a finite set and {Si}i∈I is an I-indexed collection of objects in Extr, then
X(⊔i∈ISi)
∼→∏
i∈I
X(Si).
2. X is a small colimit of representable presheaves.
Note that finite coproducts in Extr are disjoint and universal. It follows that CondAn
can also be construed as an ∞-category of sheaves of anima on Extr with respect to a
Grothendieck topology, satisfying the technical smallness condition 2. The only thing pre-
venting it from being an ∞-topos in the technical sense is that CondAn is not presentable
(because Extr is not essentially small). But this does not cause problems in practice, and
one generally has access to the whole toolkit of an ∞-topos.
An important example for us is the Postnikov tower of a condensed anima X, or more
generally the relative Postnikov tower of a map of condensed anima X →Y . Viewing a
consensed anima X as a presheaf of anima on Extr, the Postnikov tower of X is perfectly
naive: it is given by section-wise Postnikov trunctation (as this clearly preserves the sheaf
condition). The relative Postnikov tower of X →Y is hardly more complicated: its formation
15


## Page 16


commutes with base-change, so by descent one can reduce to the case of Y ∈Extr and in
that case the relative Postnikov tower agrees with the absolute one past the first stage. In
any case one easily sees that all Postnikov towers converge by reduction to the case of An.
The most basic condensed anima are in principle the objects of Extr coming via the
Yoneda embedding, but these are completely inexplicit (the basic example is the Stone-Cech
compactification of an infinite discrete set). The more general compact Hausdorff spaces give
a more practical starting point. Explicitly, there is a fully faithful embedding
CHaus →CondAn
sending K ∈CHaus to the presheaf
S ↦Cont(S,K)
on Extr.
For K ∈CHaus we can always find an S0 ∈Extr with a surjective continuous map S0 ↠K,
then an S1 ∈Extr with a surjective continuous map S1 ↠S0 ×K S0, etc., and in the end we
can find a hypercover of K in CondAn such that the terms lie in Extr. In joint work with
Scholze (see [51]), it is shown that this kind of hypercover, though completely inexplicit, can
be used to do basic calculations. In particular:
Theorem 2.2. Suppose K ∈CHaus ⊂CondAn.
1. If A is a discrete abelian group, then RΓ(K;A) (in the sense of cohomology of the
“topos” CondAn/K) agrees with the sheaf cohomology RΓshf(K;A) of the topological
space K; more precisely the comparison map
RΓshf(K;A)
∼→RΓcond(K;A)
is an isomorphism, where this comparison map comes from the fact that an open cover
is also a cover in the condensed Grothendieck topology.
2. If V is a Frechet space over R, viewed as a condensed abelian group, then
Hi(K;V ) = 0
for i > 0, and (of course) H0(K;V ) = Cont(K,V ).
Proof. For 1, see [51] Theorem 3.2. For 2, in the case V = R see [51] Theorem 3.3. The same
proof works for any R-Banach space. The passage to Frechet spaces follows by a standard
Mittag-Leffler argument.
Remark 2.3. Implicitly, in the above Hi(X;−) is viewed as a functor from condensed abelian
groups to abelian groups. But it naturally promotes to a functor Hi(X;−) from condensed
abelian groups to condensed abelian groups, where the S-valued points (for S ∈Extr) are
given by Hi(S × X;−). In other words, the cohomology naturally has condensed structure.
16


## Page 17


Since S × K ∈CHaus whenever K ∈CHaus, the above results immediately allow to read
off the condensed structure as well. For example, in 1 above the cohomology Hi(K;A) is
discrete (as a condensed abelian group) and in 2 the cohomology Hi(K;V ) vanishes as a
condensed abelian group for i > 0 and for i = 0 identifies with the (condensed abelian group
associated to the) Frechet space of continuous functions Cont(K;V ).
One can often extend these results to more general classes of topological spaces by writing
a topological space X as a union of compact Hausdorff subspaces. For example, by this
argument Claim 1 of Theorem 2.2 directly applies to arbitrary locally compact Hausdorff
spaces as well. Similarly, we can extend in a different direction, to objects like BG (viewed
as a condensed groupoid, meaning a 1-truncated condensed anima) for a locally compact
Hausdorff group G, by the bar resolution of BG. For example:
Lemma 2.4. Suppose G is a real Lie group.
1. For A a discrete abelian group, Hi(BG;A) for i ≥0 identifies with the usual cohomology
of the classifying space of the Lie group G with A-coefficients in the sense of algebraic
topology.
2. If V is a Frechet space with continuous G-action, then RΓ(BG;V ) is calculated by the
usual complex of continuous cochains from the theory of continuous group cohomology.
(If π0G is countable, this is a complex of Frechet spaces; in general it is a complex of
products of Frechet spaces.)
Proof. For 1, this follows by the usual bar resolution and the above (functorial) comparison
with sheaf cohomology, which (functorially) compares to usual cohomology. For 2, by the
same bar resolution it suffices to show that the cohomology of Gn with Frechet coefficients
vanishes in positive degrees for n ≥0. This follows from Theorem 2.2, the fact that the
connected components of Lie groups are σ-compact, and another Mittag-Leffler argument.
Remark 2.5. If K is a maximal compact subgroup of G and G is connected, then the
famous Van Est theorem shows that the cohomology in 2 is also calculated by the relative
Lie algebra cohomology of (Lie(G),Lie(K)) acting on the (Frechet) subspace of C∞-vectors
in V ; see [29], [36].
In [51] it was also explained how to leverage these kinds of cohomology calculations
into Ext calculations between various condensed abelian groups. Formally, these Ext’s are
defined to be Hom’s between shifts in D(CondAb), the derived ∞-category of condensed
abelian groups (which we in turn define to be the ∞-category of small D(Ab)-valued sheaves
on Extr). In particular, we proved the following result ([51] Corollary 4.9).
Theorem 2.6. Let A and B be locally compact Hausdorff abelian groups, viewed as condensed
abelian groups. Then:
17


## Page 18


1. Hom(A,B) identifies with the usual group of continuous homomorphisms A →B, and
more generally the internal Hom(A,B) identifies with the group of continuous homo-
morphisms A →B equipped with the compact-open topology, viewed as a condensed
abelian group.
2. Ext(A,B) identifies with the group of extensions of A by B in the exact category of
locally compact Hausdorff abelian groups.
3. Exti(A,B) = 0 for all i > 1, and if B = R/Z or B = R then also Ext1(A,B) = 0.
Remark 2.7. Implicit in 2 is the claim that the functor LCA →CondAb from locally
compact Hausdorff abelian groups to condensed abelian groups is exact. The preservation of
kernels is obvious, and the only other claim to check is that a quotient map in LCA goes to a
surjective map of condensed abelian groups (or just condensed sets). But in fact this is even
true for any open surjective continuous map of locally compact Hausdorff spaces f ∶X ↠Y ,
since such a map is “compact-covering”: for every compact subset K ⊂Y there is a compact
subset L ⊂X with f(L) = K.
In particular, we also have the following non-abelian version: if G is a locally compact
Hausdorff group and H ⊂G is a closed subgroup, then the usual topological quotient map
G →G/H of locally compact Hausdorff spaces is surjective as a map of condensed sets. We
conclude that the topological quotient G/H matches the quotient in condensed sets.
Remark 2.8. Similarly, the Ext1 comparison above implies the following: if A and B are
locally compact Hausdorff abelian groups and X is a condensed abelian group which is an
extension of B by A, then X is locally compact Hausdorff.
Again, the nonabelian generalization holds: if we have an extension of condensed groups
1 →N →G →G/N →1
such that N and G/N are locally compact Hausdorff, then so is G. This follows from an
even-more-nonabelian result: suppose X →Y is a map of condensed sets with Y locally
compact Hausdorff, such that for all S ∈Extr with a map S →Y the pullback S ×Y X is
locally compact Hausdorff. Then X is locally compact Hausdorff.
To prove the above result, working locally on Y in the topological sense we can assume
that Y is compact Hausdorff. Choose a surjection S0 ↠Y with S0 ∈Extr, so that Y is
the quotient of S0 by the closed equivalence relation S0 ×Y S0. Then setting T0 = S0 ×Y X,
we get that X is the quotient of T0 by T0 ×X T0. Now by assumption T0 is locally compact
Hausdorff; moreover by pullback we see that T0 ×X T0 ⊂T0 × T0 is represented by a closed
subset and even gives a proper equivalence relation on the locally compact Hausdorff space
T0. It follows that the topological quotient is also locally compact Hausdorff and the quotient
map is proper; in particular, it is also surjective as a map of condensed sets and hence a
fortiori the topological quotient equals the condensed quotient, proving the claim.
A corollary of the above theorem is the following “derived Pontryagin duality”.

M
∼→(M ∨)∨;

Let M ∈D(LCA). For parts 2 and 3, we need to show that M ∨∈D(LCA) and that
M
∼→(M ∨)∨.
First assume M is concentrated in degree 0 and corresponds to a locally
compact abelian group. Then by Theorem 2.8, M ∨identifies with the Pontryagin dual of
M in degree 0. Thus the claims hold by usual Pontryagin duality. By devissage, claims 2
and 3 therefore also hold for M concentrated in only finitely many degrees. Then because of
the t-exactness on LCA there is no issue in passing to the limit/colimit along the Ponstikov
tower / cotower to deduce the claim for arbitrary M.
Importantly, this corollary automatically passes to a more general situation. Suppose G
is a condensed group. We can consider the equivariant derived category
DG(CondAb)
of derived condensed abelian groups with G-action. Just as D(CondAb) can be viewed as the

DG(CondAb) →D(CondAb),
and it follows formally from slice topos nonsense that this functor commutes with internal

as well. We deduce the following:

Lemma 2.14. The full subcategory of CondAn spanned by the condensed anima of the form

Proof. Suppose (Xi)i∈I is our filtered inverse system of condensed anima. By [40] 5.3.1.18 it
is no loss of generality to assume the indexing (∞-)category I is a poset.
Let us first show that there exists a map from ∗to the inverse limit, or equivalently,
there exist compatible basepoints in all the terms in our limit diagram. To start with, fix
arbitrarily a basepoint xi ∈Xi for all i ∈I. For any j ≥i in I, let fji ∶Xj →Xi denote the
transition map in the inverse system. Then the condensed set of homotopies γ ∶fji(xj) ≃xi,
denote it Tji, is a torsor for π1(Xi,xi), hence lies in CHaus ⊂CondAn. Moreover there are
natural continuous maps

for k ≥j ≥i induced by the given homotopy fji ○fkj ∼fki.

Tabc ⊂∏

a point in ∩Tabc in particular gives a point in lim

Via these compatible basepoints, it follows that our diagram promotes to diagram of

G-torsor, and this produces the required inverse to the above map.
One last thing worth mentioning about condensed anima is that unlike in many toposes,
the terminal object ∗actually does behave like a point. More precisely, the functor
X ↦X(∗)
from CondAn to An preserves all limits and all colimits, and (hence) all Postnikov towers. In
particular, if X is a condensed anima with τ≤0X = ∗, then there is only one homotopy class
of maps ∗→X, and in particular the homotopy groups of X are well-defined (as condensed
groups) up to isomorphism in the usual way.
22


## Page 23


3
“Almost compact” condensed anima
Weil groups are not usually compact, but they are “almost compact” in the following tech-
nical sense.
Definition 3.1. Say that a locally compact Hausdorff group G is almost compact if it admits
a compact normal subgroup K such that either:
1. G = K;
2. G/K ≃(Z,+); or
3. G/K ≃(R,+).
For brevity, by an “almost compact group” we will mean an almost compact locally compact
Hausdorff group.
Lemma 3.2. Let G be an almost compact group. Then K as in Definition 3.1 is unique and
functorial (i.e. a continuous homomorphism G →G′ of almost compact groups restricts to
K →K′). Indeed K is the largest compact subgroup of G.
Proof. By a “largest” compact subgroup, we mean a compact subgroup which contains all
other compact subgroups. Then this follows from the fact that every compact subgroup of
Z or R is trivial.
Lemma 3.3. Let G be an almost compact group, with largest compact subgroup K.
1. There exists an action of G/K on K such that G is isomorphic to the semidirect product
G/K ⋉K.
2. If G/K ≃R, then there exists an isomorphism of G with the direct product R × K.
Proof. By [30], continuous homomorphisms from R lift along quotient maps (by closed sub-
groups) in the category of locally compact Hausdorff groups. The same is obviously true
for (Z,+), this being the free group on one generator. This means that G →G/N admits a
section, which as usual identifies G with the semidirect product of the resulting conjugation
action of G/N on N.
For the second claim, start from an isomorphism G ≃G/K⋉K as in 1, and view the action
of G/K on K as a continuous homomorphism G/K →Aut(K) where the target (the group
of continuous automorpishms of K) has the compact-open topology. There is a continuous
homomorphism c ∶K →Aut(K) recording the inner automorphisms; let Inn(K) denote the
(closed, as K is compact) image of this map, so that alternatively we have
Inn(K) = K/Z(K)
where Z(K) = ker(c) is the center of K (a closed subgroup). Then Inn(K) is a closed normal
subgroup of Aut(K), and (this being the main point) a theorem of Iwasawa ([33] Theorem
1) says that the quotient Out(K) = Aut(K)/Inn(K) is totally disconnected.
23


## Page 24


Since G/K ≃R is connected, it follows that our homomorphism G/K →Aut(K) lands in
Inn(K). Since again R is projective in locally compact Hausdorff groups by [30], it follows
that we can lift G/K →Aut(K) along c to a homomorphism G/K →K.
This translates into the desired statement. Let us explain this in an abstract way, without
formulas, by passing to condensed anima. An action of a condensed group H on another
condensed group H′ induces an action of H on the condensed anima BH′, and on general
grounds the quotient (BH′)/H identifies with B(H ⋉H′). The fact that the action lifts
along c ∶H′ →Aut(H′) implies that the induced action on BH′ is homotopic to the constant
action, hence the quotient (BH′)/H identifies with the quotient of the constant action, which
is BH′ × BH. Thus we get an isomorphism H ⋉H′ ≃H′ × H.
The following permanence property of almost compact groups is useful.
Lemma 3.4. Let G be an almost compact group. Then any closed subgroup H ⊂G is almost
compact, and for every closed normal subgroup N ⊂G, the quotient G/N is almost compact.
Proof. Let K be the largest compact subgroup of G. Since π ∶G →G/K is an open surjective
map of locally compact Hausdorff spaces, it is compact-covering, i.e. for any compact subset
C ⊂G/K there is a compact subset C′ ⊂G such that π(C′) = C. We deduce that π−1C = C′⋅K
is also compact, so that π is proper. It follows that π is closed. Thus π(H) ⊂G/K is closed.
As a closed subgroup of either 0, (Z,+), or R, we get that π(H) is also isomorphic to either
0, (Z,+) or R. Moreover, as π is proper, so is π∣H ∶H →π(H), and therefore π∣H is a
quotient map. Thus H ∩K ⊂H is a compact normal subgroup for which H/H ∩K = π(H)
is isomorphic to either 0, Z, or R, and hence H is almost compact, as claimed.
Now suppose N is a closed normal subgroup. The image of K in G/N is a compact
normal subgroup, and the resulting quotient G/NK is also the quotient of G/K by a closed
normal subgroup, hence is isomorphic to either 0, Z, R, or R/Z. In the first 3 cases we’re
done, and in the last case G/N is an extension of R/Z by a compact group, hence compact,
so we’re also done.
Our refinements of Weil groups will be condensed anima with fundamental group the
Weil group, and with compact Hausdorff higher homotopy. We now give some preliminaries
on the cohomology of such condensed anima, starting from the case of compact fundamental
group. From here on, when discussing cohomology we don’t distinguish notationally between
the condensed group Hi(X;A) and the underlying abstract group Hi(X;A). Rather, we will
always just write Hi(X;A), and make clear with words whether we mean to consider the
condensed structure or not.
Lemma 3.5. Let X be a condensed anima equipped with an isomorphism τ≤1X ≃BG for
some compact Hausdorff group G and such that πiX is compact Hausdorff for all i ≥2. Then:
1. For all i ≥0, the functor Hi(X;−), from condensed abelian groups with G-action to
condensed abelian groups, commutes with filtered colimits.
2. For every Frechet space V over R with continuous G-action, Hi(X;V ) = 0 for all i > 0
and H0(X;V ) = V G (a closed subspace of V , hence also Frechet).
24


## Page 25


3. For every discrete abelian group D with continuous G-action, Hi(X;D) is discrete for
all i ≥0.
Proof. First consider the case X = BG. The bar resolution shows that the cohomology of X
is the (derived) inverse limit of the cohomologies of the Gn, but in any range of degrees it’s
just a finite inverse limit. To prove 1, since filtered colimits are exact it therefore suffices to
prove it for the Gn. But these Gn are compact Hausdorff spaces and hence can be resolved
by profinite sets, for which the commutation is clear on general grounds (the Grothendieck
topology defining condensed anima is finitary). Claim 2 (again for X = BG) follows from
a Haar measure argument exactly as in the proof of 2.11. For 3, again the bar resolution
reduces us to showing the analogous claim for Hi(S;D) when S is compact Hausdorff — or
just profinite, by a further resolution. (We freely use that discrete abelian groups form a
Serre subcategory of condensed abelian groups.) But by 2.2 Hi(S;D) = 0 for i > 0 when S
is profinite, and when i = 0 it’s the internal Hom from S to D, which is discrete.
Next consider the case X = K(A,n) for n ≥1, where A is a compact Hausdorff abelian
group. The case n = 1 has already been handled, so we can proceed inductively. Again we
have a bar resolution of K(A,n + 1) = BK(A,n) which shows fairly directly that 1,2, and 3
for K(A,n) imply 1,2, and 3 for K(A,n + 1).
Now look at the general case. Evidently, by looking degree-wise we can assume X is
truncated, and then we proceed by induction on n for which X is n-truncated. The case
n = 1 is already handled, so suppose n > 1 and we know the claim for (n−1)-truncated guys.
Consider the fiber sequence
K(πnX,n) →X →τ≤n−1X.
This implies
RΓ(X;A) = RΓ(τ≤n−1X;RΓ(K(πnX,n),A))
for any condensed abelian group A with G-action. Thus 1 for X follows from 1 for K(πnX,n)
and 1 for τ≤n−1X, and similarly for 2 and 3.

Starting from Proposition 3.6 we can also analyze the cohomology of many other co-

To analyze it in terms of coefficient systems to which the above

with trivial π1X-action. Obviously, Z is discrete and R is Frechet. Then Proposition 3.6

Corollary 3.7. Let X be a condensed anima such that τ≤1X = BG for some almost compact

1. H0(X;R/Z) = R/Z;

pact Hausdorff abelian group);

Using this, we have the following interpretation of the usual procedure of building anima
step-by-step via their Postnikov tower.
Lemma 3.11. Let G be a locally compact Hausdorff group. For n ≥1, let Xn(BG) denote
the ∞-groupoid of n-truncated condensed anima X equipped with an isomorphism τ≤1X ≃BG
such that πiX is compact Hausdorff for all i ≥2. Note that τ≤n induces a natural map
Xn+1(BG) →Xn(BG).
For x ∈Xn(BG) corresponding to X →BG, the ∞-groupoid of lifts of x to Xn+1(BG)
identifies with the ∞-groupoid of pairs (A,κ) where A is a compact Hausdorff abelian group
with continuous G/G○-action and
κ ∶X○→K(A,n + 2)
is a G/G○-equivariant map of condensed anima, recalling that X○∶= X ×BG BG○.
More
precisely, this identification is induced by sending a pair (A,κ) as above to the condensed
anima Y = fib(κ)/(G/G○), equipped with its tautological map to X○/(G/G○) = X, which
induces the required τ≤nY ≃X.
28


## Page 29


Proof. We build the map backwards using the Postnikov tower of (Y →BG) ∈Xn+1(BG) in
condensed anima over BG (see [40] 6.5 and 7.2), or equivalently by descent, the Postnikov
tower of Y ○→BG○in G/G○-equivariant condensed anima over BG○.
The G/G○-equivariant map Y ○→τ≤nY ○= X○is an n + 1-gerbe. Thus there is a unique
G/G○-equivariant abelian group object A in (relatively 0-truncated) condensed anima over
X○such that Y ○→X○promotes to an n + 1-gerbe banded by A. A relatively 0-truncated
anima over X○uniquely descends to τ≤1X○= BG○, so we can equivalently treat A as a
G/G○-equivariant abelian group object over BG○. Next we claim it is uniquely pulled back
from a G/G○-equivariant abelian group object over ∗, i.e. a condensed abelian group A with
G/G○-action, and that this condensed abelian group A is compact Hausdorff.
For this, note that on pullback along any point x of X (unique up to homotopy, as
π0X = ∗and ∗is projective in condensed anima), A gives πn+1(X,x), which is compact
Hausdorff by assumption. A G/G○-equivariant abelian group object over τ≤1X = BG○is
the same as an abelian group object over (BG○)/(G/G○) ≃BG. Thus A corresponds to a
compact Hausdorff group A with continuous G-action. Since the automorphism group of a
compact abelian Hausdorff group is totally disconnected, the action must be trivial on G○.
Therefore, the G-action descends (uniquely) to a G/G○-action, and this proves the claim
(that A is uniquely pulled back from a G/G○-equivariant compact Hausdorff abelian group
A).
We deduce that Y ○→X○has the structure of an n + 1-gerbe banded by this A. Thus by
[40] Theorem 7.2.2.6 it is uniquely classified by a map
X○→K(A,n + 2).
The universal n+1-gerbe banded by A corresponds to the canonical base-point of K(A,n+2),
so this translates to the desired statement.
To finish this section, we discuss phenomena related to filtered inverse limits of these
“almost compact” condensed anima, again starting with the compact case. For motivation,
we recall the following well-known fact (for which we provide a proof since we couldn’t find
one in the literature):
Lemma 3.12. The functor from the pro-category of compact Lie groups to the category of
compact Hausdorff groups, given by sending a pro-system to its inverse limit, is an equiva-
lence.
Proof. Let G be a compact Hausdorff group. By the Peter-Weyl theorem, L2(G) is the
Hilbert space direct sum of finite-dimensional unitary representations of G. It follows that
G maps by an injective continuous homomorphism to a product of unitary groups. Thus G
is isomorphic to a closed subgroup of a product ∏i∈I Hi of compact Lie groups. For a finite
subset I0 ⊂I, let GI0 be the image of G under the projection ∏i∈I Hi →∏i∈I0 Hi. Then G is
the filtered inverse limit of the GI0, and each GI0 is a compact Lie group because a closed
subgroup of a Lie group is a Lie group.
Thus the functor from the pro-category of compact Lie groups to compact Hausdorff
groups is essentially surjective. To show fully faithfulness, we need to see that if (Gi)i∈I is a

We can view an object C ∈CondAn with π0C = ∗and πnC a compact Lie group for all
n ≥1 as some kind of differential refinement of its homotopy type ∣C∣and ask, what kinds of
more standard geometric structures on a connected topological space will give rise to such a
refined homotopy type?
More precisely, we recall (c.f. [50] Lemma 11.9) that if X is, e.g. a topological space
homotopy equivalent to a CW-complex, then its associated condensed set X admits an
initial map to an anima ∣X∣, and this anima is the usual homotopy type of X. One can ask
what structure on a connected X will naturally produce a C as above and a map
X →C
which induces an isomorphism on ∣⋅∣.
Example 3.19. Let us consider the simplest nontrivial example, that of an n-sphere Sn
with n odd. The homotopy groups of Sn are finite except in degree n where πnSn ≃Z. Our
desired C should therefore have the same homotopy groups as Sn except in degrees n −1
and n, where we should have πnC = 0 and πn−1C = R/Z.
I claim we can build a canonical such C plus a map Sn →C as above given any choice of
smooth structure and Riemannian metric on Sn (or really, just the associated volume form).
Let or denote the orientation local system on Sn (it is trivial, but never mind that). As is
well-known, the Riemannian metric gives rise to a canonical volume form, which is a global
section of the sheaf
Ωn ⊗or
on Sn. This sheaf canonically maps to the shifted twisted de Rham complex Ω●⊗or[n], and
even to
Fib(Ω●⊗or[n] →Ω0 ⊗or[n]).
Up to quasi-isomorphism, the source is Rδ ⊗or[n]; on the other hand the target maps to
R ⊗or[n] because smooth functions yield continuous functions.
Thus we have a section of the family of Eilenberg-Maclane spaces K(Rδ,n) over Sn
twisted by the orientation sheaf, together with a nullhomotopy of the induced section of
K(R,n). This gives rise to the desired data. Indeed, if we forget the twists for simplicity,
then we get
Sn →K(Rδ,n) →K(R,n)

C = fib(∣Sn∣→K(R,n)).
If G is a compact Lie group acting freely on Sn by isometries (or, just preserving the
volume form), then since the above was canonical we get an induced
Sn/G →C/G
which provides a solution to our problem for X = Sn/G as well. For example, when n = 3 we
can take

(which is the norm-1 subgroup of WR) acting by left multiplication on the unit quaterions S3
with their canonical invariant metric and in this way we get a canonical “compact homotopy
type” for X = RP2.
4
Moore anima
We set ourselves the following problem: for an almost compact group G (Definition 3.1), we
want to modify the condensed anima BG by adding higher homotopy in order to simplify
the cohomology. More precisely, we make the following definition.
Definition 4.1. Let G be a locally compact Hausdorff group. A Moore anima for G is a
condensed anima X equipped with a map t ∶X →BG satisfying the following properties:
1. The map t induces an isomorphism τ≤1X ≃BG.
2. For all i ≥2, the homotopy (condensed) group πiX is compact Hausdorff.
3. We have
Hi(X○;R/Z) = 0
as a condensed abelian group for all i ≥2, where (see Definition 3.9) X○= X ×BG BG○.
Remark 4.2. Our main case of interest is when G is almost compact; then the condensed
abelian group Hi(X○;R/Z) is discrete for i ≥2 by Corollary 3.7, so in 3 above, vanishing in
the condensed sense and vanishing in the ordinary sense are equivalent. This discreteness is
also what motivates the requirement that the πiX be compact Hausdorff, as we will eventu-
ally see that the homotopy groups are determined as Pontryagin dual to such cohomology
groups, e.g.
π2X = H3(BG○;R/Z)∨.
Example 4.3. Suppose G is totally disconnected. Then BG○= ∗, and it follows that BG
itself (or, more precisely, the identity map BG →BG) is already a Moore anima for G in this
sense. In fact the ∞-groupoid of Moore anima for G is contractible, as we will see from the
general analysis. Thus the theory in this section is trivial in the totally disconnected case.
Later we will formulate a stronger Moore-like property for which the theory is interesting
even when G is totally disconnected.
37


## Page 38


The method for producing Moore anima (or more precisely, for analyzing the ∞-groupoid
of Moore anima) will be via the familiar procedure of obstruction theory from homotopy
theory, based on the Postnikov tower. In this we follow [62] on equivariant Moore spaces,
but transported to the condensed context.
We start with a truncated variant of Definition 4.1.
Definition 4.4. Let G be a locally compact Hausdorff group and n ≥1. An n-stage Moore
anima for G is a condensed anima X equipped with a map t ∶X →BG satisfying the following
properties:
1. X is n-truncated: X
∼→τ≤nX.
2. The map t induces an isomorphism τ≤1X ≃BG.
3. For all i ≥2, the homotopy (condensed) group πiX is compact Hausdorff.
4. Letting X○= X ×BG BG○, we have Hi(X○;R/Z) = 0 when n + 1 ≥i > 1.
Formally, we can allow n = ∞: an ∞-stage Moore anima for G is just a Moore anima for
G in the sense of Definition 4.1.
Remark 4.5. Naively, it would seem more natural to take the weaker version of 4 where
you only ask cohomology vanishing up to degree n, not n + 1. But the above form of the
definition makes the obstruction theory much simpler.
Proposition 4.6. Let G be a locally compact Hausdorff group.
1. Let 1 ≤n < ∞, and let Y →BG be an n + 1-stage Moore anima for G. Then X ∶=
τ≤nY →BG is an n-stage Moore anima for G.
2. Let t ∶X →BG be a map of condensed anima. Then t is a Moore anima for G if and
only if for all 1 ≤n < ∞, the induced map τ≤nX →BG is an n-stage Moore anima for
G.

∼→BG, and that πiX is
compact Hausdorff for all i ≥2. Note that, since BG and BG○are 1-truncated, we have
τ≤n(Y ○) = X○.
Since K(R/Z,i) is i-truncated, it follows immediately from the corresponding fact for Y ○that
Hi(X○;R/Z) = 0 for n ≥i > 1. The only nontrivial point is to show that Hn+1(X○;R/Z) = 0.

where A is a compact Hausdorff abelian group (isomorphic to πn+1Y ○). Consider the as-

E2-page which contribute to Hn+1(X○;R/Z) are the
Hi(K(A,n + 2);Hj(Y ○;R/Z))

the usual unitary character group of the locally compact Hausdorff group G○.

Theorem 4.15. Let G be a locally compact Hausdorff group, let n ≥1, and let t ∈Mn(BG)
be an n-stage Moore anima for G, namely t ∶X →BG is a condensed anima over BG
satisfying the axioms in Definition 4.4. Let H ∶= Hn+2(X○;R/Z) be the indicated cohomology
group, with its natural G/G○-action inherited from X○= X ×BG BG○.
Then there is a canonical map
σ ∶H →cG[n + 3]
in DG/G○(CondAb) (with H in degree 0) such that the anima of lifts of t to Mn+1(BG)
identifies with the anima of null-homotopies of σ.
Proof. Consider M ∶= τ≥0RΓ(X○;R/Z[n + 2]) as an object in DG/G○(CondAb) concentrated
in degrees 0 through n + 2. By definition of an n-stage Moore anima, we have
τ≥1M = cG[n + 2].
On the other hand, by definition of H we have
τ≤0M ≃H.
Thus the boundary map for the cofiber sequence
τ≥1M →M →τ≤0M
gives a map
σ ∶H →cG[n + 3]
of the correct form.
To show it has the indicated property, note that a null-homotopy of σ is the same thing
as a splitting of the above cofiber sequence. But this is the same as a map H →M such
that the composition with M →H0M = H is the identity map. If we define the compact
Hausdorff G/G○-module A by
A ∶= H∨,
then by Lemma 3.10 this is also the same thing as a map
κ ∶X○→K(A,n + 2)
for which κ∗as in Lemma 4.12 is an isomorphism. Thus the statement follows from Lemma
4.12.

5
Background on class formations
The notion of class formation arose as an axiomatization of the cohomological approach to
class field theory. We recall one form of the classical definition from [3]:
Definition 5.1. A class formation is a pair (Γ,A) where:
1. Γ is a profinite group;
2. A is a discrete abelian group with continuous Γ-action,
such that the following conditions on group cohomology are satisfied, for every open subgroup
H ⊂Γ and every open normal subgroup N ⊂H:
44


## Page 45


1. H1(H/N;AN) = 0;
2. H2(H/N;AN) is cyclic of order [H ∶N].
The point of the notion of class formation is that if we give ourselves a bit of extra
structure, namely a collection of generators (uH,N)H,N for these cyclic groups H2(H/N;AN),
then via the Tate-Nakayama lemma ([59] IX) we deduce that cup product with uH,N induces
an isomorphism in Tate cohomology
̂Hi(H/N;Z)
∼→̂Hi+2(H/N;AN)
for all i ∈Z. In particular, when i = −2 we get
(H/N)ab ≃AH/tr(AN).
If we assume suitable compatibility properties for these uH,N under inflation and restrction
(see [59] XI), then we can pass to the limit over N to get the associated Artin maps
AH →Hab
which have dense image with respect to the topology on profinite group Hab, and satisfy
various natural compatibilities as H varies over open subgroups of Γ. In this way we can
think of a class formation as providing abelian groups which naturally refine the Hab as H
varies over open subgroups of Γ.
In practice, Γ will be an absolute Galois group. In particular, it will not actually be
canonically defined as a profinite group, but only as a connected profinite groupoid, or
equivalently a Galois category in the sense of [27]. To make our discussions more canonical,
we therefore switch to that language.
Definition 5.2. A class formation (reformulated) is a pair (C,A) where:
1. C is a Galois category;
2. A is a sheaf of abelian groups on C,
such that the following conditions are satisfied, for every connected object X ∈C and every
subgroup G ⊂Aut(X):
1. H1(G;A(X)) = 0;
2. H2(G;A(X)) is cyclic of order #G.
It is clear from Grothendieck Galois theory that, if we fix a fiber functor on the Galois
category C, or equivalently, if we fix a profinite group Γ and an identification of C with the
category of finite continuous Γ-sets, then the notions of class formation in Definitions 5.1
and 5.2 are one-to-one equivalent: the data and axioms line up.
In the following section we aim to explain how to get Weil groups, or rather (to say things
more canonically) Weil groupoids, from class formations, essentially retelling part of [3]. But
45


## Page 46


for that it’s not enough to just have a class formation: one needs extra data. At a first level,
one needs chosen compatible generators for the groups H2(G;A(X)), exactly as is required
to get Artin maps following Tate-Nakayama as above. But actually one needs more, namely
some cocycle-level version of such data, in order to canonically build the corresponding
“group extensions”, not just up to isomorphism.
In fact, such cocycle level lifts also show up in another place in the theory, at least
implicitly, namely in the proof of Tate-Nakayama. Given a finite group G, a G-module A,
and a class u ∈H2(G;A), then one way to describe the Tate-Nakayama argument is that one
interprets u ∈Ext2
G(Z,A) instead; then one represents u by a map
Z →A[2]
in D(Z[G]) and passes to the homotopy fiber. For this fiber one has the hypothesis that its
Tate cohomology vanishes in two consecutive degrees for all subgroups H ⊂G, and one wants
to conclude it vanishes identically. The point in the end (after some standard devissage to
reduce to p-groups, then to cyclic groups of order p) is that the Tate cohomology of cyclic
groups is 2–periodic, so that vanishing in two consecutive degrees is equivalent to vanishing
identically.
Now, the datum of a cocycle-level lift of the group cohomology class u is the same as the
datum of the above map in the derived ∞-category of G-modules, and it is also the same as
the datum of the fiber object, equipped with isomorphisms H0 ≃Z and H1 ≃A. All of these
(and more) are just different ways of talking about gerbes over BG banded by A. As usual
in mathematics, however, the interpretation which is closest to linear algebra is the easiest
one to work with. In the end we are led to the following.
Definition 5.3. A refined class formation is a triple (C,F,α) where:
1. C is a Galois category;
2. F ∶C →D(Z) is a co-sheaf such that F(X) is concentrated in the range of degrees
[0,1] for all X ∈C;
3. α is a map H0F →Z of functors C →Ab to the constant functor with value Z, such
that α(X) is an isomorphism for all connected X ∈C.
We will show that if (C,F,α) is a refined class formation, then H1F, which is a co-presheaf
on C, also carries the canonical structure of a sheaf via the formalism of transfer maps, and
that (C,H1F) is a class formation. Conversely, we will explain what it takes to promote a
class formation to a refined class formation.
For this we use three fairly standard lemmas, for which our basic references will be
[13] and [14]. The first explicates sheaves/cosheaves on Galois categories attached to finite
groups. Every Galois category is canonically the filtered union of Galois categories each of
which is equivalent to the Galois category of a finite group, so this is also useful for general
Galois categories.
46


## Page 47


Lemma 5.4. Let G be a finite group, and let C(G) denote the Galois category of finite
G-sets. Let D be an ∞-category which admits all finite coproducts and all H-orbit objects
for all subgroups H ⊂G. Then restriction along the inclusion BG ⊂C(G) of the G-torsors
induces an equivalence
CoSh(C(G);D)
∼→Fun(BG;D)
with inverse given by left Kan extension.
Dually, if D admits all finite products and all H-fixed point objects, then restriction along
BG ⊂C(G)op induces an equivalence
Sh(C;D)
∼→Fun(BG;D)
with inverse given by right Kan extension.
Proof. It suffices to treat the dual assertion about sheaves and right Kan extensions. Then
for d ∈D, consider the composition hd ○F, where hd = Map(d,−) ∶C →An. By definition
F is a sheaf if and only if hd ○F is a sheaf for all d ∈D. On the other hand the pointwise
criterion for right Kan extension shows that F is the right Kan extension of its restriction
to a given full subcategory if and only if the same is true for hd ○F for all d ∈D. Thus we
reduce to the case D = An, where the assertion is given by [13] Example 4.4.
The second result explains the mechanism of “transfer maps”, which provide a canonical
presheaf structure on any cosheaf and dually a canonical pre-cosheaf structure on any sheaf.
Lemma 5.5. Let C be a Galois category, and let D be a semi-additive ∞-category which
admits G-orbit and G-fixed point objects for all finite groups G. Denote by Span(C) the span
category ((2,1)-category, actually) of C, where the objects are the objects of C, the maps from
X to Y are the spans
X ←M →Y
in C, and composition is given by pullback. Note that there are obvious functors
C →Span(C)
and
Cop →Span(C),
sending each object to itself and sending a map X →Y to the span X = X →Y (resp.
Y ←X = X). Then:
1. Let Funcoshf(Span(C),D) denote the full subcategory of functors F ∶Span(C) →D such
that the restriction along C →Span(C) is a cosheaf. Then we have
Funcoshf(Span(C),D)
∼→coSh(C;D)
via restriction along C →Span(C).

2. Let Funshf(Span(C),D) denote the full subcategory of functors F ∶Span(C) →D such
that the restriction along Cop →Span(C) is a sheaf. Then we have
Funshf(Span(C),D)
∼→Sh(C;D)
via restriction along Cop →Span(C).
3. Let F ∈Fun(Span(C),D). Any two of the following three properties imply the third:
(a) F ∈Funcoshf(Span(C),D);
(b) F ∈Funshf(Span(C),D);
(c) for all connected X ∈C and all subgroups G ⊂Aut(X), the natural “transfer”
map F(X)G →F(X)G, associated to the induced G-action on F(X), is an iso-
morphism.
Proof. Claims 1 and 2 are dual, so it suffices to treat 2. Let N(C) denote the poset of
isomorphism classes of connected Galois objects in C, and for N ∈N(C) consider the full
subcategory CN ⊂C consisting of objects which can be covered by a finite disjoint union of
objects in the isomorphism class N. The poset structure is such that
N ≤N ′ ⇔CN′ ⊆CN.
Note that if C = C(Γ) for a profinite group Γ, then N(C) is the poset of open normal subgroups
of Γ with poset structure given by inclusion, and CN identifies with the Galois category of
finite Γ/N-sets.
We have a filtered colimit
C = lim
Ð→
N
CN
of finitary sites (c.f. [13] 3.1) and CN is equivalent to the Galois category of finite G-sets
for some finite group G. It follows readily that the claim in 2 reduces to the case when
C = C(G) for some finite group G. Then by Lemma 5.4 it suffices to show that restriction
along BG ⊂Cop →Span(C) induces an equivalence
Funshf(Span(C),D)
∼→Fun(BG;D).
Note by [13] Prop. 4.2 that the sheaf property for a presheaf F ∶Cop →D breaks into two
parts:
1. F preserves finite products;
2. F(X/H)
∼→F(X)H for all connected X ∈C and all subgroups H ⊂Aut(X).
Assuming 1 (F preserves finite products), we can (following [13] Prop. 4.11) rephrase 2 as

̃
