Now consider 3.
If F satisfies two of the three conditions
(a),(b),(c), then in particular F is either a sheaf or a cosheaf, and therefore F is additive.
Thus we can restrict our attention to additive functors F ∈Fun⊕(Span(C),D). Now, for
connected X ∈C and G ⊂Aut(X) a subgroup, consider the composition
F(X)G →F(X/G) →F(X)G,
where the first map uses the co-presheaf structure and the second map uses the presheaf
structure. Then F is a cosheaf if and only if the first map is an isomorphism for all (X,G),
and F is a sheaf if and only if the second map is an isomorphism for all (X,G). Thus by the
2 of 3 property for isomorphisms it suffices to show that the composition of the above two
maps identifies with the transfer as in (c), for any F ∈Fun⊕(Span(C),D). In other words,
we need to see that the G × G-equivariant map
F(X) →F(X/G) →F(X)
identifies with ∑g∈G g. But this follows by definition of composition in the span category.
In particular, given a cosheaf on C, we get a canonical presheaf structure with the same
values on objects (uniquely extend the cosheaf to the span category by 1, then restrict along
Cop →Span(C)), and this presheaf will be a sheaf if and only if the transfer-isomorphism
condition 3(c) holds. We formalize this as follows.
Definition 5.6. Let C be a Galois category, D a semi-additive ∞-category which admits
G-orbit and G-fixed point objects for all finite groups G, and let F ∈coSh(C;D). Define the
presheaf
F tr ∈Fun(Cop,D)
as the image of F under the composition
coSh(C;D) ≃Funcoshf(Span(C),D) →Fun(Cop,D),
where the first map is the inverse to the isomorphism of 1 of Lemma 5.5 and the second map
is composition with the natural map Cop →Span(C) described in Lemma 5.5.
Dually, if F is a sheaf we get a co-presheaf F tr. Note that by construction F and F tr
agree on objects; moreover the association F ↦F tr is functorial in C (for exact functors)
and D (for additive functors), via composition.

The third standard lemma is a version of the Tate-Nakayama lemma.
Lemma 5.9. Let G be a finite group, and let D a Z-linear stable ∞-category equipped with a
t-structure and admitting H-fixed point and H-orbit objects for all subgroups H ⊂G. Suppose
M ∈Fun(BG;D) is an object of D equipped with G-action such that M is concentrated in a
bounded range of degrees (with respect to the t-structure). Then the following properties are
equivalent:
50


## Page 51


1. For all subgroups H ⊂G, the object MH (homotopy orbits for the H-action, or H-
hyperhomology) is concentrated in a bounded range of degrees.
2. For all subgroups H ⊂G, the object M H (homotopy fixed points for the H-action, or
H-hypercohomology) is concentrated in a bounded range of degrees.
3. For all subgroups H ⊂G, the natural transfer map MH →M H is an isomorphism.
Under these conditions, if M is in degrees [a,b], then so are M H and MH for all H ⊂G.
Proof. Because 1 and 2 are dual to each other and 3 is self-dual (with respect to D ↔Dop),
it suffices to show that 2 and 3 are equivalent. Suppose 3 holds. If M is in degrees [a,b],
then M H is in degrees ≤b and MH is in degrees ≥a. As MH ≃M H by hypothesis, this
implies M H is concentrated in degrees [a,b], i.e. condition 2 holds (moreover we get the final
claim). For the converse 2 ⇒3, we follow the standard Tate-Nakayama argument.
We want to show 2 ⇒3.
Replacing D by Ind(D0), where D0 is the smallest stable
subcategory of D containing all M H for H ⊂G and closed under t-truncation, we can
assume that D is presentable. It follows by the multiplicative transfer machinery in [14] that
M tH ∶= cofib(MH →M H)
is a module over ZtH ∈D(Z). In fact the assignment G/H ↦M tH organizes into a functor
on the span category of finite G-sets as in the previous lemma, and even in that context it
is a module over G/H ↦ZtH which is a ring in a natural way.
Now, first suppose H is cyclic. Then the standard resolution implies that ZtH is periodic.
But assuming 2, M tH vanishes in low enough degrees; therefore, being a module over a
periodic ring spectrum, it must be 0. This proves that 2 ⇒3 when G is cyclic. By the
standard induction with a nontrivial central subgroup, we deduce from this that 2 ⇒3 also
holds if G is nilpotent. Next, note that for a general finite group G and an object M ∈D
with G-action and subgroup H ⊂G, there are maps M tG →M tH and M tH →M tG such
that the composition M tG →M tG is multiplication by #[G ∶H]. This follows from the
multiplicative span category formalism referred to above, using the corresponding (obvious)
fact for G/H ↦ZH.
In total, we deduce that M tG is killed by multiplication by [G ∶H] for all nilpotent
subgroups H ⊂G. By the Sylow theorems there is no prime number which divides all such
indices, and it follows that M tG = 0. Replacing G by a subgroup we deduce the claim.
Now we will finally explain the relationship between class formations and refined class
formations.

Remark 5.13. We can summarize points 1 and 2 as follows: a (refined) class formation
on a profinite group canonically induces (refined) class formations on any open subgroup
or quotient group. Note that, in fact, the data of a (refined) class formation on a profinite
group is equivalent to the data of compatible (refined) class formations on each of its finite
quotients; this is obvious from the definitions.

Definition 5.14. A condensed refined class formation is a triple (C,F,α) consisting of a

refined class formation, together with the structure of condensed sheaf on the “class group”
sheaf A, produced by the formalism of transfers exactly as in the non-condensed case.

An isomorphism between two algebraic closures induces an isomorphism of refined
class formations, inducing the identity on underlying class formations; this map from
isomorphisms of algebraic closures to isomorphisms of honest class formations is sur-
jective, and two isomorphisms induce (canonically) equivalent isomorphisms of refined
class formations if and only if they differ by a power of Frobenius. This explains the
̂Z/Z-ambiguity.
It is unclear to the author whether one can expect a canonical choice of refined class
formation in this setting, or equivalently, if one can expect a canonical choice of alge-
braic closure of Fp “up to powers of Frobenius”. (It is also unclear to the author how to
make the word “canonical” precise, so as to turn this into a well-defined mathematical
question.)

see that giving a refined class formation for R is equivalent to giving a representative

incorporate Hermitian data in the categories.

1
2Z-graded C-vector spaces V equipped with a semi-linear automorphism σ such that
σ2 = (−1)2λ in graded degree λ. Again this is also known as the local Kottwitz category
for R, see [35], [32].

One thing missing from the above discussion of examples is the quite nontrivial question

To address this we need to explain the notion of a morphism of refined class formations.

subject to the condition that α′ ○f♮should agree with the map induced on f∗by α ∶F →Z.

We already saw above that the datum of the local Kottwitz gerbes is equivalent to the da-
tum of refined local class formations (or equivalently local Weil groupoids); now we see that
the datum of global Kottwitz gerbes does not correspond directly to that of refined global
class formations (or global Weil groupoids): rather, the data of global Kottwitz gerbes plus
compatibility with the local Kottwitz gerbes is equivalent to the data of global refined class
formation plus compatibility with the local refined class formations. In the above presenta-
tion, the local data is contained in FA, the global Kottwitz data together with compatibility
is encoded in the map FKott →FA, the global Weil data together with compatibility is en-
coded in the map FA →FQ, and the equivalence between the Kottwitz and Weil data in total
is an instance of the axiom of a stable ∞-category that cofiber and fiber sequences are the
same thing. Of course in some sense this all goes back to Nakayama-Tate, [65]. For a more
explicit cocycle-based discussion of the same situation, see also [54].
Remark 5.23. It causes some philosophical discomfort that the above data appears to not
be canonical. It’s also annoying that the “construction” of this data is so indirect. Here is a
clean statement which would automatically give all of the above structure. Let NRing denote
the full subcategory of locally compact Hausdorff commutative rings spanned by the rings
commonly used in number theory: the global and local fields, their rings of (S-)integers, and
the finite fields. Then all of the above-discussed structure (refined class field theory for all
number-theoretic rings, plus local-global compatibility) amounts to the following data:
1. A functor F ∶NRingop →D(Z) with values concentrated in degrees [0,1];
2. An isomorphism H0F ≃Z to the constant functor with value Z;
3. An isomorphism H1F(R) ≃CR to the usual class group assigned to R, such that the
functoriality in R gives the “usual” maps between class groups.
69


## Page 70


This functor F should satisfy the property that it gives a cosheaf on the finite etale site of
any number ring, and that its restriction to such a finite etale site recovers “the” refined
class formations as discussed above.
Wouldn’t it be nice if there were some direct construction of such a functor F? The
article [11] goes part of the way towards this, by showing that R ↦K1(lcR) (the algebraic
K1-group of the category of locally compact Hausdorff R-modules) serves as the appropriate
H1F (see also [8]). However K0(lcR) is far from being constant with value Z, so a serious
modification of this idea is required to realize the above hope.
Possibly this is too much to expect, even just for finite fields. But for number fields there
is a posteriori a canonical such functor by Example 5.16, so it makes sense to ask to describe
it directly, without resorting to cohomological analysis. This is surely a difficult problem,
but one could start just with cyclotomic fields where special features may be helpful.
Remark 5.24. For those who prefer sheaves to cosheaves, it may make sense to remember
the condensed structure on F and pass to the Pontryagin dual. Then you’re looking for a
derived sheaf with H0 = R/Z and H−1 the Pontryagin dual of the class group. Note that this
derived sheaf is almost discrete. Actually, in the number field setting, if we use the compact
form of the class groups and take the fiber of the map from R in degree 0, we see that the
data we’re looking for is just a derived sheaf with Z in degree 1 and the (discrete) dual to
the norm-1 idele class group in degree −1: entirely discrete, but it completely recovers global
class field theory.
6
From refined class formations to Weil groupoids
Now we turn to the problem of assigning Weil groupoids to refined class formations, essen-
tially redoing the analysis in [3] from a slightly different perspective. First let’s discuss a
more basic instance of the same idea: associating Galois groupoids to Galois categories. We
know from Grothendieck Galois theory that that the 2-category of Galois categories (objects:
Galois categories; 1-morphisms: exact functors; 2-morphisms: natural transformations) is
a (2,1)-category which identifies with the opposite of the (2,1)-category whose objects are
the profinite groups G, whose morphisms are the continuous homomorphisms G →G′, and
whose set of 2-morphisms
φ0 ≃φ1 ∶G →G′
between two such homomorphisms is the set of g′ ∈G′ such that φ1 = g′φ0(g′)−1. This same
2-category can also be realized as the full subcategory of CondAn spanned by the objects of
the form BG where G is a profinite group.
Our first order of business is to give a more direct description of this fully faithful em-
bedding. This is based on the following standard definition.
Definition 6.1. A map of condensed anima f ∶X →Y is finite etale if, locally on Y in the
condensed Grothendieck topology, it is isomorphic to a map of the form I × Y →Y where I
is a finite set.
70


## Page 71


Remark 6.2. It follows from descent theory that finite etale maps X →Y in CondAn are
classified by maps
Y →fSet≃≃⊔
n≥0
∗/Σn
in CondAn.
Example 6.3.
1. Suppose S is an extremally disconnected profinite set. By definition
every cover of S in the condensed Grothendieck topology is refined by a finite disjoint
union decomposition of S. It follows that the finite etale maps X →S are exactly the
finite disjoint unions of clopen subsets of S.
2. Suppose S is a compact Hausdorff space. By non-abelian cohomological descent applied
to a hypercover by extremally disconnected profinite sets, we deduce that maps S →BG
in CondAn for any group G are the same as global sections Γ(S;BG) in the sense of
non-abelian sheaf cohomology. It follows that every finite etale cover of S is locally
trivial in the open cover topology of S, and hence that finite etale maps to S correspond
to finite-sheeted covering spaces in the usual sense from point-set topology.
3. Suppose G is a locally compact Hausdroff group. Then finite etale maps to BG identify
with finite sets with continuous G-action. This is obvious by descent along ∗→BG.
4. For a general condensed anima S, every finite etale map X →S uniquely descends to
the Postnikov truncation τ≤1S.
For S ∈CondAn, the full sub-∞-category
fEtS ⊂CondAn/S
is a 1-category which admits all finite limits and colimits. (The above inclusion preserves
finite limits; for colimits in general one has to apply the relative Postnikov truncation τ≤1
to the colimit in CondAn/S, but for quotients by finite groups acting faithfully this last
truncation is unnecessary.) If T →S is a map, then pullback induces a functor
fEtS →fEtT
which is exact, i.e. preserves all finite limits and colimits. This category fEtS satisfies all
the same exactness properties as the category of finite sets, if these are expressed in terms
of interactions between finite limits and finite colimits. All of this is general topos-theory
nonsense, using descent and the fact that base-change functors preserve finite limits, all
colimits, and relative Postnikov truncations (which can be built in terms of finite limits and
geometric realizations).
The only thing preventing fEtS from being a Galois category is the potential lack of
fiber functor. In other words, fEtS is the more general kind of Galois-type category, which
corresponds to a profinite groupoid not necessarily having a unique isomorphism class of
objects.
We will not need to go into the details of this; we simply make the following
defintion.

Definition 6.4. Suppose C is a small category admitting all finite limits and colimits. De-

functors
C →fEtS .

for an arbitrary S ∈CondAn we have
C(S) = Funex(C,fEtS).
Proposition 6.6. The above functor restricted to those C which are Galois categories,
C ↦C ∶GalCatop →CondAn,
has the following properties:
1. It is fully faithful.
2. For a profinite group Γ, its value on the Galois category of finite continuous Γ-sets
identifies with BΓ.
3. On the essential image of this functor (which is the full subcategory of CondAn spanned
by the above BΓ) the inverse functor is given by sending S ↦fEt/S.
Proof. It is tautological from descent that the functor C ↦C admits the left adjoint
S ↦fEtS .
In particular, for a small category with finite limits and colimits C we have an obvious exact
comparison functor

and for a condensed anima X we have an obvious map
X →fEtX.
Consider now the case of the Galois cateogry CΓ of fintie continuous Γ-sets for a profinite
group Γ. As discussed above we have fEtBΓ ≃CΓ, which gives a natural comparison functor
BΓ →CΓ,
and to prove all the claims it suffices to show this is an isomorphism for all Γ. Note that
both functors Γ ↦BΓ and Γ ↦CΓ commute with filtered inverse limits. For the latter this
is clear, and for the former see Lemma 2.14. Thus we can reduce to the case where Γ is a
finite group, where we need to see that giving a Γ-torsor over a profinite set S is the same
as giving an exact functor from finite Γ-sets to fEt/S. This is standard Galois theory.

such that F(X)/G
∼→F(X/G) for all (X,G) as in 2. But by definition, when F is a refined

tr→F(X)G
where the composition is the transfer map for the G-action on F(X). The assumptions

Definition 6.9. Let C = (C,F,α) be a condensed refined class formation, and suppose G ∶

natural in U ∈Cconn.

Remark 6.11. Evidently, this condensed anima WC is 1-truncated (i.e. it is a condensed

to the condensed groupoid C from Definition 6.4.

Corollary 6.19. The functor sending a condensed refined class formation C = (C,F,α) to

for some connected V ∈C mapping to U. (This property is the “existence theorem”
for the underlying class formation.)

every open subgroup of W of finite index contains φ−1Γ′ for some open subgroup Γ′ ⊂Γ.
However, every open subgroup of W contains (φ−1N)c for some open normal subgroup N

as required.
Conversely, suppose we have W satisfying 2(a) and 2(b).
Setting Γ = ̂
W we get a
homomorphism φ ∶W →Γ which satisfies the properties 2(a),(b),(c) of the previous theorem,
and therefore corresponds to a condensed refined class formation, for which we need only
check the existence theorem. But an open subgroup of H1F(U) = H/Hc can be viewed as
an open subgroup of W/Hc when H ⊂W is the open subgroup corresponding to U, and for
this open subgroup the property 1(c) is tautological.
Thus nice enough refined class formations can equivalently be viewed as certain condensed
anima. This applies to all of the number-theoretic examples of class formations discussed
above in Example 5.16, and the corresponding condensed anima is BW where W is “the”
Weil group.
In these number theoretic examples, we have a dichotomy: the underlying condensed
class formations (C,A) have either one or the other of the following properties.
1. For all connected U ∈C, the condensed abelian group A(U) is an extension of Z by a
profinite abelian group (called the Z-case in [64]);
2. For all connected U ∈C, the condensed abelian group A(U) is an extension of R by a
compact Hausdorff group (called the R-case in [64]).
The Z-case occurs in all non-archimedean settings (finite fields, nonarchimedean local
fields, and function fields), while the R-case occurs in all archimedean or mixed settings
(archimedean local fields and number fields). It is formally convenient to also allow a third
simpler case, the compact case, when A(U) is compact Hausdorff for all U. Thus we end up
with the following trichotomy.
Definition 6.20. Let C be a condensed refined class formation which satisfies 1(a)(b)(c)
from Corollary 6.19. We say that:
82


## Page 83


1. C is of R-type if H1F(U) is an extension of R by a compact Hausdorff group for all
connected U ∈C;
2. C is of Z-type if H1F(U) is an extension of Z by a profinite group for all connected
U ∈C;
3. C is of compact type if H1F(U) is compact Hausdorff for all connected U ∈C.
For brevity, if we say that C is a refined class formation of R-type, resp. Z-type, resp.
compact type, we mean that C is a condensed refined class formation satisfying 1(a)(b)(c)
from Corollary 6.19 as well as 1 resp. 2 resp. 3 just above.
To finish this section we analyze the Z-case; this amounts to a reprise of a special case
of a theorem of Brumer relating class formations to the condition of strict cohomological
dimension 2 for a profinite group, [9] Theorem 1.
Proposition 6.21. The equivalence in Corollary 6.19 restricts to an equivalence between
the following two (2,1)-categories:
1. The category of refined class formations of Z-type;
2. The full subcategory of CondAn spanned by those condensed anima of the form BW,
where W is of the following type: take a profinite group Γ of strict cohomological
dimension 2 and a surjective homomorphism Γ ↠̂Z and set
W = Γ ×̂Z Z.

the associated condensed refined class formation is given by F = RΓ(−;R/Z)∨without any
truncation needed.

profinite completion of H1F(U) for all connected U.

Remark 6.22. Contained in the above analysis was the following: refined class formations
of profinite type (i.e. of compact type and such that the class group A(U) is profinite for all
U) are in 1-1 correspondence with profinite groupoids BΓ such that Γ is a profinite group
of strict cohomological dimension ≤2. As mentioned, modulo language this is a theorem of

7
Weil-Moore anima
We finished the previous section with a result (Proposition 6.21) showing that in the Z-case,
refined class formations correspond to profinite groups of strict cohomological dimension
2 equipped with a surjection to ̂Z. Part of the reason this is so nice is that we get the
refined class formation directly from the R/Z-cohomology (of finite etale covers) without
any truncation, the key point being that this cohomology automatically vanishes outside
cohomological degrees 0 and 1.
This fails drastically in the R-case.
Example 7.1. Consider the refined class formation for the local field C: the Galois category
is trivial, and the corresponding Weil groupoid is BC×. The R/Z-cohomology of BC×, in
degrees ≥2, matches the Z-cohomology up to a shift by Lemma 3.7, which matches the
usual Z-cohomology of BS1 ∼CP∞in algebraic topology by Lemma 2.4. Thus we have
nonvanishing of R/Z-cohomology in all odd degrees.
We view this as a defect: our perspective in this paper is that all reasonable class for-
mations should arise via the untruncated cohomology of some object; this should be the
ultimate explanation for why the (co)sheaf property holds on the full derived level even
though the refined class formation only lives in two degrees. In the Z-cases this object is the
Weil groupoid, but in the other cases we need to add higher homotopy groups. We will use
the formalism for doing this from Section 4, leading to the following definition.
Definition 7.2. Let C be a refined class formation which is either of Z-type, of R-type, or of
compact type (see Definition 6.20), with associated Weil groupoid WC ≃BW from Corollary
6.19. A Weil-Moore anima for C is a condensed anima X equipped with an isomorphism
t ∶τ≤1X ≃WC
such that (X,t) is a Moore anima in the sense of Definition 4.1, i.e. (to recall):
1. πiX is compact Hausdorff for all i ≥2;
85


## Page 86


2. Hi(X○;R/Z) = 0 for all i ≥2, where X○= X ×BW BW ○and W ○is the connected
component of the identity in W.
We now show that if (X,t) is a Weil-Moore anima, then the condensed anima X by itself
canonically recovers the class formation via untruncated cohomology, as desired.
Lemma 7.3. Let C be a refined class formation which is either of R-type, of Z-type, or of
compact type, and suppose given a Weil-Moore anima (X,t) for C.
Then:
1. for every finite etale Y →X, we have Hi(Y ;R/Z) = 0 for all i ≥2;
2. the refined class formation C is recovered as
C = (fEtX,RΓ(−;R/Z)∨,ϵ)
where ϵ is induced by (−) →∗.

This
truncated cohomology is the same for WC as for X because of the datum t. On the other

Finally we can prove the main theorem.
Theorem 7.6. Let C be a condensed refined class formation which is either of Z-type, of R-
type, or of compact type. Then there exists a Weil-Moore anima (X,t) for C, and moreover
such a Weil-Moore anima is unique up to isomorphism.
In the Z-type case and the profinite case it is completely unique (contractible space of
choices).
Proof. Let WC ≃BW denote the associated Weil groupoid (Corollary 6.19). By definition,
a Weil-Moore anima for C is the same thing as a Moore anima for BW. In the Z-type and
profinite cases, W is totally disconnected, so the space of such Moore anima is contractible
by Example 4.17. In the R-type and compact-type cases, because of Corollary 4.16 and
Proposition 7.4, we need only show H2(BW ○;R/Z) = 0. But W ○is abelian by construction,
so this follows from Lemma 4.11.
Remark 7.7. Let X be a Weil-Moore anima as above and A a compact abelian group with
W = π1X-action. By Lemma 3.10 we have

Hi(X;A) = 0

The Weil-Moore analogs of some theorems of Tate
We will now prove the analogs of some fundamental theorems of Tate in Galois cohomology
([63]) for these new objects, the Weil-Moore anima. Our focus is on the cohomology of
Weil-Moore anima with discrete coefficients, often restricted to be p-power torsion for some
fixed prime p. The first theorem expresses that Weil-Moore anima have strict cohomological
dimension 2 with discrete coefficients, modulo some technicalities in the Z-cases.
Theorem 8.1. Let X be a Weil-Moore anima with underlying class formation either of Z-
type, R-type, or compact type, and let W = π1X denote “the” associated Weil group, with
profinite completion ̂
W (the Galois group). If X is of Z-type, assume the same additional
technical assumption from Proposition 7.4 is satisfied. Then for any discrete group M with
W-action, we have:
1. Hi(X;M) = 0 for i > 3;
2. If either M comes from a ̂
W-action or M is torsion, then H3(X;M) = 0.
Proof. We recall from Remark 7.7 that the cohomology with compact Hausdorff coefficients
vanishes in degrees ≥3. Now we must move to discrete coefficients.
97


## Page 98


Suppose M comes from a ̂
W-action; thus we need to show cohomology vanishing in
degrees > 2. By Lemma 3.6 cohomology on X commutes with filtered colimits, so we reduce
to the case where M is a finitely generated discrete ̂
W-module; in particular M is finitely
generated as an abelian group. Consider the exact sequence
0 →Mtors →M →M ⊗R →M ⊗(R/Z) →0.
We have cohomology vanishing in degrees > 2 for M ⊗(R/Z) and Mtors because these are
compact, and we have cohomology vanishing in degrees > 1 for M ⊗R by Lemma 3.6. We
deduce cohomology vanishing in degrees > 3 for M. Now we need to improve this to get
cohomology vanishing for degree 3 as well. Since M is finitely generated, it is a quotient of
a finite direct sum of copies of Z[W/H] for some open subgroup H ⊂W of finite index. By
cohomology vanishing for the kernel in degree 4, we deduce that the vanishing claim for M
is implied by that for Z[W/H]. Thus we need
H3(Y ;Z) = 0
for all connected finite etale Y →X (corresponding to H ⊂W via Galois theory). But by
Lemma 3.7 this is equivalent to H2(Y ;R/Z) = 0, which holds because that cohomology group
is Pontryagin dual to H2 of our refined class formation hence vanishes by definition.
If X is of compact type or R-type, then every discrete abelian group M with continuous
W-action comes from a ̂
W-action, as ̂
W = W/W ○. Thus we are done in those cases. In the
Z-case, we have a short exact sequence
1 →W 1 →W →Z →1
where W 1 is a profinite group of cohomological dimension ≤1 with torsion discrete coefficients
(hence ≤2 with arbitrary discrete coefficients), so we conclude because in any case Z has
cohomological dimension 1.
Remark 8.2. The analogous result to 2 for Galois cohomology with restricted ramification
(with odd-primary coefficients, or for number fields with no real places, these being “obvious”
necessary restrictions) is conjectured to be true, but is currently unknown, and in fact it
turns out to be equivalent to Leopoldt’s conjecture. See [45] for a thorough discussion. In
the Weil-Moore setting, we avoid Leopoldt’s conjecture.
Having discussed cohomological dimension properties, we now turn to finiteness proper-
ties, in the setting of p-primary torsion coefficient systems.

We discuss the examples from number theory, to see when this p-finiteness holds. Note

finitely generated abelian groups and uniquely p-divisible groups, since those clearly have
the desired mod p finiteness property.
Example 8.6.

generally we can easily see that the same conclusion holds as long as the characteristic
of K is different from p.
Thus, for a nonarchimedean local field K, the corresponding Weil-Moore anima XK
has the p-finiteness property whenever K has characteristic ≠p. (This is of course just
a restatement of the usual finiteness of Galois cohomology in these cases.)

Following Tate, whenever this p-finiteness holds we can ask about the Euler characteristic.
More precisely, given a p-power torsion E1-ring R and an M ∈Perf(R) with ΩX-action
(assumed to descend to ̂

The magic of Tate’s formulas is that in number-theoretic situations, this “Euler charac-
teristic” only depends on the class [M] ∈K0(R), forgetting the ΩX-action. (Rather, in the
original setting of Galois cohomology, this is only true up to archimedean corrections; but
passing to Weil-Moore anima fixes this.) In topological situations, say for a local system on
a finite CW-complex, this kind of Euler characteristic formula is simple to explain, because
we can break our space up into contractible pieces. But for, e.g. a general profinite group
with p-finiteness it can easily fail: see the treatment by Serre in his article on Euler-Poincar´e
measures, [57], which analyzes this phenomenon. Here we rephrase Serre’s arguments, but
avoiding the scaffolding of Euler-Poincar´e measures.

Remark 8.12. We have just seen that Tate’s global Euler characteristic formula, in our
setting of Weil-Moore anima, becomes the statement that XK,S, the Weil-Moore anima with
restricted ramification, has Euler characteristic 0. This is the same answer as one gets in
the function field case, but there we have a much nicer explanation, given by Kato ([34]): if
C denotes the associated affine curve over Fp, then given (for example) a lisse Qℓ-sheaf V
on C, not only is its cohomology on C finite dimensional, but it is also finite dimensional on
C = C ×Fp Fp. Then the cofiber sequence
RΓ(C;V ) →RΓ(C;V )
1−φ

immediately gives vanishing of the Euler characteristic over C.
Actually, it gives more, namely it gives a canonical trivialization of RΓ(C;V ) as a point
in K(Qℓ). On the other hand, if it happens to be the case that RΓ(C,V ) = 0, then we clearly
get another completely obvious trivialization. Comparing the two trivializations gives a well-
defined element in K1(Qℓ) = (Qℓ)×. Up to sign conventions, this identifies with the value of
the associated L-function at s = 0. Other L-values can be obtained by twisting by powers
of the cyclotomic character. All but finitely many of such twists will have vanishing RΓ,
and in this way one gets a “meromorphic function on the weight space”, and this is just the
L-function of V .
Conjecturally, according to Kato, there should similarly be a more refined version of
the calculations proving Tate’s global Euler characteristic formula, yielding not just a proof
that it vanishes in K-theory, but a specific trivialization in K-theory (or at least, in the
τ≤1-truncation of K-theory); moreover, after varying in a weight space which includes points
where the cohomology vanishes, this should explain the existence of p-adic L-functions. In
other words, in the language of Beilinson ([4]), Kato’s Iwasawa Main Conjecture predicts an
“animation” of Tate’s Euler characteristic formula, and says that a p-adic L-function “is”
such an animation.

The last result we want to discuss is Poitou-Tate duality. But it will help to set up a bit
of formalism of coefficient objects first. We will work in the Z-linear context, and we take
the following definition.
Definition 8.22. Let X be a Weil-Moore anima, or more generally a condensed anima with
π0X = ∗.
1. Define a condensed coefficient system on X to be an object M in the ∞-category
D(CondAn/X) of unbounded derived abelian group objects in the slice topos CondAn/X;
that is, D(CondAn/X) is ∞-category of small D(Z)-valued sheaves on the site Extr/X
of extremally disconnected profinite sets with a map to X.
2. Define a discrete coefficient system to be a condensed coefficient system such that the
object on D(CondAb) obtained by pulling back under (any choice of) ∗→X is discrete,
i.e. lies in D(Z) ⊂D(CondAb).
Denote the full subcategory of discrete coefficient systems by
Det(X;Z) ⊂D(CondAn/X).
Remark 8.23. If we fix ∗→X, giving the condensed group ΩX, then D(CondAn/X) is the
same as the ∞-category of ΩX-equivariant derived condensed abelian groups
DΩX(CondAb)
which we considered in Section 2. Thus, informally, Det(X;Z) is the ∞-category of discrete
derived abelian groups with continuous ΩX-action.
112


## Page 113


Remark 8.24. By general topos theory, D(CondAn/X) has a natural t-structure, has all
colimits, and has a natural tensor product; moreover all of these features are preserved under
pullback, in particular under the map ∗→X. Note that, over ∗, all these operations preserve
the full subcategory D(Z) ⊂D(CondAn). It follows that Det(X;Z) is closed under colim-
its, tensor products, and t-truncations inside D(CondAn/X), and that all these operations
commute with pullback along ∗→X viewed as a conservative functor
Det(X;Z) →D(Z).
The heart of Det(X;Z) identifies with the abelian category of discrete abelian groups with
continuous π1X = W-action.
Remark 8.25. If we write X as a (small) colimit of extremally disconnected profinite sets,
we can view Det(X;Z) as the corresponding limit of the usual (unbounded derived) sheaf
categories on profinite sets.
We will not use this remark, except perhaps to note that
Det(X;Z) is a presentable ∞-category.
Remark 8.26. Suppose R is an E1-Z-algebra and M ∈D(R). By definition, to give an
N ∈ModR(Det(X;Z)) whose pullback along ∗→X gives M is the same as to give a pointed
map of condensed anima
X →B AutR(M)
where AutR(M) is the condensed automorphism object of M as an R-module.
If M ∈

discrete anima. For example, if R is an animated commutative ring, then local systems of
R-modules on X of rank d can either be understood as objects in ModR(Det(X;Z)) whose
underlying object in D(R) lies in Vectd(R) or as being classified by maps of condensed anima
X →B GLd(R)
where the target is discrete.
We have the following basic results allowing to work with these coefficient systems; com-

Lemma 8.27. Let X be a Weil-Moore anima, with underlying class formation of Z-type,
R-type, or compact type. Then:
1. The global sections functor Det(X;Z) →D(CondAb) lands in D(Z) and preserves
colimits.
2. For every connected finite etale Y →X, the object Z[hY ] ∈Det(X;Z) corepresenting
sections over Y is a compact object.
If Y →X corresponds to the open subgroup
H ⊂W = π1X of finite index, then Z[hY ] lives in degree 0 and corresponds to Z[W/H]
as a W-module.

Remark 8.31. In particular, this applies when Y is the Weil-Moore anima of a number

Corollary 8.32. Suppose X and Y are Weil-Moore anima such that Y is of compact type

This means that for the purposes of discrete torsion coefficients, we can ignore the possible

examples, whenever Y is of Z-type then so is X, and hence we are anyway able to aovid the
nonuniqueness of Weil-Moore maps, even before passing to profinite completion.)
Finally, we turn to duality. We aim to prove a version of Poitou-Tate duality for Weil-
Moore anima. We start with local fields, where in the nonarchimedean case the result is of
course just equivalent to usual local Tate duality by Lemma 8.17. We will give a proof from
the Weil-Moore perspective, as a warm-up for the global case. The key thing to understand
turns out to be the following: if F is the cosheaf giving the refined class formation, then
there are two natural ways to build a sheaf from F, namely taking by applying the covariant
operation (−)tr or by applying the contravariant operation (−)∨, and at the level of linear
algebra local Tate duality will amount to the assertion that mod p, the two resulting sheaves
are isomorphic up to a twist and shift. But we will set things up in a more standard way.

## Page 125


Remark 8.36. We chose the above formulation of Poitou-Tate duality, in the setting of a
finite set S of places, to highlight the analogy with 3-manifolds. But the duality becomes
picturesque in a slightly different manner if we pass to the situation where S consists of all
places, so we are interested in the Weil-Moore anima XK of the number field itself. In this sit-
uation, given R and M as above, the analog of the “boundary cohomology” ⊕ν∈SRΓ(XKν;M)
is given by the adelic construction
RΓ(XAK;M) ∶= lim
Ð→
S0
∏
ν∈S0
RΓ(XKν;M) × ∏
ν/∈S0
RΓ(XOν;M),
where S0 runs over finite subsets of places (of the form considered in Example 7.12, say).
The object on the right can be canonically viewed as a filtered system of Pro-objects in
Perf(R), such that each transition map in the filtered colimit has cofiber lying in Perf(R) ⊂
Pro(Perf(R)); as such it has the canonical structure of a Tate R-module, or rather a Tate
object in Perf(R) in the sense of [28]. We recall that:
1. Tate(R) contains both Ind(Perf(R)) and Pro(Perf(R)) as full subcategories, and is
generated by these;
2. Maps X →Y where X and Y each live in one of these full subcategories (but not
necessarily the same one) are the “obvious” formal ones;
3. The intersection of these two subcategories identifies with Perf(R) (sitting inside each
in the obvious natural way);
4. The R-linear duality Perf(R)op ≃Perf(Rop) naturally extends to Tate(R)op ≃Tate(Rop)
and interchanges Ind(Perf(−)) and Pro(Perf(−)).
On the other hand, RΓ(XK;M) can be naturally viewed as lying in Ind(Perf(R)) hence
also in Tate(R), by writing XK = lim
←ÐS0 XK,S0 which gives a colimit on RΓ. By the same
token there is a natural restriction map
RΓ(XK;M) →RΓ(XAK;M)
in Tate(R).
Then the statement of Poitou-Tate duality, at this level, is that the fiber RΓc(XK;M) of
above map lies in Pro(Perf(R)) ⊂TateR, and the resulting cofiber sequence
RΓ(XK;M) →RΓ(XAK;M) →RΓc(XK;M)[1]
has the following self-duality: the R-linear dual of this cofiber sequence for M identifies with
the same cofiber sequence but for M ∨(1)[2]. On the middle term this follows from local
Tate duality, and for the outer terms it follows from global Tate duality (in the form proved
above).

## Page 126


From this statement one can, following Nekovar ([44]) extract dualities in Perf(R) by a
formal procedure. Suppose given a “Selmer structure” S for M, meaning a cofiber sequence
C →RΓ(XAK;M) →D
where C ∈Pro(Perf(R)) and D ∈Ind(Perf(R)) (so, opposite to the situation of RΓ(XK;M)
mapping to RΓ(XAK;M), where something discrete maps with compact quotient). Then
applying R-linear duality and local Tate duality we get a “dual Selmer structure” S∗on
M ∨(1)[2] given by
D∨→RΓ(XAK;M ∨(1)[2]) →C∨.
If we define the Selmer cohomology to be
RΓS(XK;M) ∶= C ×RΓ(XAK ;M) RΓ(XK;M),
then it formally follows that RΓS(XK;M) ∈Perf(R), and we have the following duality:
RΓS(XK;M)∨= RΓS∗(XK;M ∨(1)[2]).
In the above proofs of Poitou-Tate duality we used a bit of abstract formalism that we
now should justify.
The situation is the following: let C be a Galois category and F a
D(Z)-valued sheaf on C. Consider the functor
M ↦RΓ(C;M ⊗F)
from Sh(C;D(Z)) to D(Z), where ⊗means sheafified derived tensor product over Z. Com-
posing with the Yoneda embedding, we get a co-presheaf
U ↦RΓ(C;Z[hU] ⊗F)
on C with values in D(Z). On the other hand, as we described in Definition 5.6, the formalism
of transfer maps also naturally produces a copresheaf Ftr from F, taking the same values as
F. The claim is that these two copresheaves are naturally isomorphic.
Lemma 8.37. Let C be a Galois category, D a Z-linear presentable ∞-category, and F ∈
Sh(C;D). Then there is a natural isomorphism between the following two functors C →D:
1. The functor U ↦Γ(C;Z[hU] ⊗F);
2. The functor Ftr from Definition 5.6.
Proof. By definition of (−)tr, it suffices to show that the first functor extends to a functor
Span(C) →D
whose restriction along Cop →Span(C) identifies with F.
126


## Page 127


Such an extension comes as follows: we apply the transfers construction to the cosheaf
U ↦Z[hU] to get a functor Span(C) →Sh(C;D(Z)), which one can then compose with
RΓ(C;−⊗F). To identify the restiction along Cop →Span(C) with F, it suffices to show that
that the presheaf given by (U ↦Z[hU])tr identifes with the presheaf given by U ↦Z[hU]∨
(meaning the Z-linear dual). However both these objects live in degree 0 so this is elementary
to check: one shows that the canonical self-duality of the finite free Z-module on a finite set
implements the desired identification.
Remark 8.38. Although D(CondAb) is not presentable for technical reasons (there is no
small set of generators), the conclusion still holds for D = D(CondAb): for example one can
reduce to the case D = D(Z) by checking on S-valued points for all extremally disconnected
S. (Recall that taking S-valued points preserves all limits and colimits.)
Remark 8.39. We restricted to the Z-linear context for familiarity, but the same claim
holds in the more general semi-additive context. One can still check by hand because the
base-case involves free E∞-spaces on finite sets, which are still just 1-truncated.
Remark 8.40. Suppose, in the situation of Lemma 8.37, that Ftr is actually a cosheaf. For
example, this happens if D = D(Z) and F takes values in some bounded range of degrees,
by Lemma 5.9. It follows from left Kan extension that Ftr extends uniquely along Yoneda
to a colimit-preserving Z-linear functor
Sh(C;D(Z)) →D,
which we will continue to denote
G ↦Ftr(G).
Left Kan extension from Lemma 8.37 then provides a natural transformation
Ftr(−) →RΓ(C;−⊗F)
which is an isomorphism on the Z[hU].
We claim that, at least in the situation where D = D(Z) and F takes values in a
bounded range of degrees, the above natural transformation is actually an isomorphism
on all G ∈Sh(C;D(Z)). As in the previous remark, we can deduce the analogous claim for
D = D(CondAb) by evaluating on extremally disconnecteds.
To prove this, by the above it suffices to show that RΓ(C;−⊗F) preserves colimits. By
the criterion as in [13] Prop. 4.12, for that it suffices to show that for all X ∈C connected,
all subgroups G ⊂Aut(X), and all M ∈D(Z) with G-action, the transfer map
(M ⊗F(X))G →(M ⊗F(X))G
is an isomorphism. (For then it follows that one iteration of the Cech construction suffices to
sheafify the presheaf tensor product, and moreover it also follows that the Cech construction
preserves colimits sectionwise, and combing we get the desired claim.) By Lemma 5.5 we
have that F(X) has vanishing H-Tate cohomology for any subgroup H ⊂G; let us show
127


## Page 128


more generally that the desired conclusion holds with F(X) replaced by any N ∈D(Z[G])
living in bounded degrees satisfying this vanishing hypothesis in Tate cohomology.
By the usual transfer tricks we reduce to the case of G a p-group for some prime p.
Resolving N by injective Z[G]-modules, we can reduce to the case where N lives in degree
0. Then by the Nakayama-Rim theorem ([59] Chapter IX), N has a two-term projective
resolution, so we reduce to the case when N is a free Z[G]-module, which is trivial.
To finish, let us use this to give a somewhat different perspective on Poitou-Tate duality,
by explaining how Poitou’s general duality theorem for class formations ([46]) looks from
the perspective of Weil-Moore anima.
Theorem 8.41. Let X be a Weil-Moore anima with underlying class formation either of
Z-type, R-type, or compact type. Let F denote the cosheaf on C = fEtX giving the condensed
refined class formation, and let F tr denote the sheaf obtained by passing to transfer maps.
Then for discrete abelian group M with ̂
π1X-action, corresponding on the one hand to a
coefficient system on X and on the other hand to a sheaf of abelian groups on C, there is a
natural map
RΓ(C;M ⊗F tr) →RΓ(X;M ∨)∨.
Here (−)∨means Pontryagin duality, and on the left we have derived sheafified tensor product
over Z.
Moreover, this map is an isomorphism whenever M is finitely generated (as an abelian
group).
Proof. We have a tautological pullback functor π−1 from fEtX to condensed anima over X,
which therefore induces a pullback on D(Z)-valued sheaves. We follow this with Pontryagin
duality, then global sections, then internal Hom to R/Z again, getting the Z-linear functor
F ↦RΓ(X;(π∗F)∨)∨
from Sh(C;D(Z)) to D(CondAb). When we specialize to F = Z[hU] for U ∈fEtX = C, we
get the value
RΓ(U;(Z)∨)∨= RΓ(U;R/Z)∨= F(U)
Thus, the co-presheaf underlying this functor is F, which happens to be a cosheaf. We
deduce by left Kan extension a natural transformation
F(−) →RΓ(X;(π∗−)∨)∨
of Z-linear functors Sh(C;D(Z)) →D(CondAb) which is an isomorphism on all Z[hU].
Applying Remark 8.40, we can rewrite this as a natural transformation
RΓ(C;(−) ⊗F tr) →RΓ(X;(π∗−)∨)∨
which is an isomorphism on all Z[hU]. An arbitrary finitely generated M admits a co-free
resolution where each term is finite direct sum of copies of Z[hU] or Z/NZ[hU] (for N ∈Z),
so we deduce the natural transformation is an isomorphism on all finitely generated M as
well.
128


## Page 129


For example, when we take X = XK,S for a number field K and a finite set of places S
containing all archimedean places and all places above some fixed prime p, then from this it
is simple to deduce the following version of Poitou-Tate duality
RΓ(XK,S;M ∨)
∼→RΓc(BGK,S;M(1)[3])∨
relating Weil-Moore cohomology to Kato-style compactly supported Galois cohomology, for
p-primary torsion coefficient systems on BGK,S. This is of course also a consequence of
Theorem 8.35 and Lemma 8.19.
